If one asks, "If that is so, why were the forms of deities, meditation on them, and so forth taught?", some say that it was for the sake of worldly accomplishments, that is, only for the sake of those who desire them. As it is said:

"So that beginners among sentient beings,
by meditating on the body of the deity,
may accomplish every activity,
it was first definitively taught in the tantras."

Others say it is solely for the purpose of making one fit for the wisdom of suchness. Still others say that by meditating on the forms of glorious Heruka and the rest, just that much alone is accomplished, as it is said: "Infinite awakening has been taught by the Sugata."

None of these views, and others like them, holds good. For the Blessed One (the Buddha), by his nature of great bliss, of one taste with emptiness endowed with the supreme of all aspects, is what is to be accomplished and what is to be meditated upon. The mere aspect is certainly not what is to be meditated upon. Nor is it possible for the mere nature of reality to stand apart, off to one side of the wheel of aspects. Whatever isolated thing becomes the object of meditation, meditating on that isolated thing is precisely not meditating on the meaning of suchness. As Āryadevapāda said:

"The deluded who meditate on non-entity
rely on something isolated;
it has the character of the space of the childish,
and that becomes the very height of inert matter."

Āryapāda, too, said:

"Whoever meditates on the nature of reality
as an inferior thing called 'unborn,'
called 'empty,' or called 'selfless,'
that is not meditation on it."

Therefore, having first made the mind stable on the mere form of the deity, one is to meditate, through the guru's instructions, solely on the nature of great bliss, whose nature is the nondual union. So how could there be any room for the faults that were stated? Moreover, what was said earlier, that one does not even attain samādhi, is not true; for it is established by experience that a mind made especially exalted through habituation to devotion and the like does attain stability on its object of focus.

As it is said:

"Therefore, whether real or unreal,
whatever has become thoroughly familiar,
when the power of meditation is perfected,
yields as its fruit a clear, nonconceptual cognition."

And from the tantra, too:

"With whatever meditation
people's minds are fully joined,
through that they take on its nature,
just like a jewel of many forms."

That even for what is not of a single aspect, seeing and hearing free of conceptual thought become vividly clear at once, just as they are, is likewise established by experience alone. Since all phenomena have as their body merely the nature of mind, what contradiction is there in it becoming clear even through meditating on a universal monarch and the like? As Sarahapāda, too, said:

"The one mind alone is the seed of all existence,
and from it nirvāṇa too radiates forth;
to that which, like a wish-fulfilling jewel,
grants the fruit one desires, I bow."

In the way of mantra, the path too is solely of the nature of meditation. Even for those who abide in the way of the perfections and accept a path whose nature is action, everything is fully completed solely by what is of the nature of meditation, because moral discipline and the rest do not depend on external things. As it is said:

"Where could fish and the like be driven
so that they would not be killed?
It is from attaining the mind of abstention
that the perfection of moral discipline is held to be."

And with regard to the perfection of generosity, too, it is said:

"The mind that gives away all one's possessions,
together with their fruits, to all people,
is declared the perfection of generosity;
therefore it is mind itself."

It is clearly understood that the accumulation of wisdom comes about through mere meditation. Therefore it is said, "Meditation alone completes the accumulations of merit and wisdom." As Āryapāda said:

"The Great Vehicle, great in its very nature,
is the accumulations of merit and wisdom;
for that reason the state of omniscience
is attained in this very life."

Likewise:

"This conviction is something quite different,
the practice toward awakening is different,
the purity of the mind's aspect is different too,
and here the fruit is seen to be different."

And also:

"Just as a vine grows up fully
and is fully endowed with flowers and fruit,
so perfect awakening in an instant
is the full arising of the two accumulations."

For that very reason, the consequence that it would remain only for a short time does not follow either, because it is precisely what reverses that which arises from clinging to what is not real. Sounds and the like, too, by being endowed with color and so forth, like the forms of the hands, feet and the rest, do not withdraw from the mind but simply occur in it. Thus, too, the eyes of the Blessed One also hear, and also meditate, and so on at length. And from the tantra, too:

"Everywhere endowed with hands and feet,
everywhere with eyes, heads and faces,
everywhere endowed with hearing in the world,
it abides pervading all."

Even though it has form, there is no fault of its not being pervasive, because form, too, is pervasive. As it is said in the Blessed Lady: "Because form is infinite, the Perfection of Wisdom is infinite," and so forth. A pot is called non-pervasive because it arises from clinging to imagined object and subject, not merely because it has form.

The various forms come about through the way of the perfection of aspiration made by great compassion of one taste with emptiness, not through meditating on mere emptiness. Who could refute that the forms of glorious Cakrasaṃvara and the rest are to be meditated upon just so, and that various forms are thereby accomplished? The apparent contradiction with scripture comes from not fully understanding its intent.

Thus, the passage beginning "Thus," whether scripture or the words of the accomplished masters, was spoken with reference to those who cling to the deity's form and the like alone as though it were reality itself; for in reality it is of the nature of the nondual two truths. And apart from that, liberation is not tenable either. As it is said in the Questions of the Four Goddesses:

"Among the eighty-four thousand
collections of the Great Sage's Dharma,
for those who do not know suchness,
all of them are without fruit."

Indrabhūtipāda, too, said in his extensive explanation:

"All that is mistaken about suchness,
through the thorough abandonment of what is imagined."

And Sarahapāda, too:

"Though one should enter beneath the earth,
or even travel through the sky,
with conceptions of separate forms
what hope is there of liberation?"

If it is objected, "Then this would lack the purpose of meditating on elaborated forms," it is not so, because of the samādhi of nondual union that is to be accomplished. Moreover, the aggregates, elements and sense sources themselves are accomplished as the nature of great bliss of one taste with suchness. As it is said in the glorious King of the Two Examinations:

"Bliss is black, bliss is yellow,
bliss is red, bliss is white,
bliss is green, bliss is blue,
bliss is all that moves and does not move.
Bliss is wisdom, bliss is method,
bliss is known as born of union,
bliss is entity, bliss is non-entity,
bliss is known as Vajrasattva."

Someone may say: "Those who cultivate only those natures of union through the guru's instructions, discarding all elaboration, may do so if they are able. But if one who is engaged only with coarse objects is not able, it is not known how such a person is to be brought into such a supremely subtle form." If one answers, "Through the guru's instructions alone," then these would be matters to be taken merely on faith. For it is untenable, by reasoning and by scripture alike, to say that there is no meditation on completion. Thus, in the glorious King of Tantras in Two Examinations, Vajragarbha asked:

"This yoga of the completion stage
is known as supreme bliss, great bliss.
If in completion there is no meditation,
what need is there for generation?"

The Blessed One declared:

"Ah, great bodhisattva,
by the very force of faith this has gone astray."

So it says; and further:

"Since without the body there is no bliss,
bliss could not even be spoken of.
Through the nature of pervaded and pervader,
beings are pervaded by bliss.
Just as the fragrance residing in a flower
is not perceived without the flower,
so without form and the rest
bliss itself would not be perceived."

And likewise:

"Since what is dependent has no power of its own,
bliss comes from the form of the deity."

And:

"Therefore the Buddha is not a non-entity,
nor is he the form of an entity either;
he has form, with the aspect of arms and faces,
and is formless, supreme bliss."

Āryadevapāda, too, had just this meaning in mind when he said in the Blending of Sūtra and Tantra: "An ordinary being like myself, who from beginningless time, through clinging to various external entities and non-entities, has clung to conceptions that fully apprehend causes whose nature is the habitual tendencies of nonexistence, existence, oneness, manyness, both, neither, not existing, not not-existing, permanence and impermanence, and who wishes to train in the samādhi of the completion stage: should such a one train in stages, or is it to be made clear in an instant through the guru's instruction alone?" The vajra master declared: "One should enter in stages and train, not all at once," and so forth. From these words it is to be understood that only those who are for the most part free of conceptions of entity, non-entity and the like, that is, those who have attained the noble grounds, are fit to cultivate the completion stage. Mere conviction, however, is not in conflict even for the other kind of person.

Moreover, Tantipa and others, speaking with regard to the power of the guru, said: "Because there is no habituation, because the path is not understood from this, because it is inexpressible, because it is sudden, because it is without afflictions, and because it is not always hidden, whatever mere self-awareness there is when all the senses have been stopped through the joy of the guru's word, that is blessing." This too is to be understood with regard to a person who has gathered such accumulations of merit and wisdom; otherwise a single Tathāgata alone would liberate all sentient beings.

Therefore the stages of habituation are just those taught in the Two Examinations and elsewhere. On that it is said:

"Through the yoga of the generation stage,
the one who keeps the vows is to meditate on elaboration;
having made elaboration like a dream,
by means of elaboration make it free of elaboration.
Thus, just as with an illusion, just as with a dream,
just as with existence in the intermediate state,
in just that way the maṇḍala becomes clear
through the practice of constant habituation."

Likewise, in the Later Tantra of the Guhyasamāja, the generation stage, known as the tantra of method, is taught as having the nature of the basis, because it is the cause of the arising, remaining and increase of the completion stage. Ḍombipāda, too, taught this very meaning:

"The first task of those who meditate is the generation stage, O lords of fine understanding;
mudrā, mantra and all the purifying recitations need not be spelled out here.
Having gained outstanding wisdom, then again, with that same one-pointed mind and fine intelligence,
through the concentration that cultivates the completion stage, ever joyful, one destroys existence."

That in the state of the completion stage the meditator of the generation stage abides without interruption, through the wheel of forms being of one taste with emptiness, whose nature is great bliss, is also to be clearly understood from the verse of glorious Hevajra beginning "Just as with an illusion." In the Ḍākinī Vajrapañjara, too:

"O friend, like the moon in water,
it is neither false nor not false;
the maṇḍala wheel is like that:
the body is pure by nature."

And what Kṛṣṇapāda said:

"Freed from the wheel of gods and goddesses
in which the tathāgatas delight,
free of the maṇḍala wheel,
one abides in the coemergent instant,"

is said because, once one has become independent, there is no further mental engagement.

Again, if it were otherwise, in what respect would this be superior to the way of the perfections? For the completion stage would then be so called merely through meditating on what is free of elaboration, and that would be the same in both. As it is said in the Dhāraṇī of the Infinite Gates:

"Think of what is to be thought as unthinkable;
do not think of the inconceivable itself;
not thinking at all of what is to be thought,
from that the dhāraṇīs are obtained."

Here too, in the thirty-third chapter:

"When one knows reality,
all thoughts are unthinkable;
where one thinks of the unthinkable,
then it becomes inconceivable,"

and so forth. In Lūyipa's Abhisamaya, too:

"That which is unthinkable yet to be thought
cannot be pointed out even to the mind;
that, too, is the inconceivable hero;
from that the dhāraṇīs are obtained."

In the Perfection of Wisdom, too: "Whoever practices yoga on space, and practices yoga on what has no covering, practices yoga on the Perfection of Wisdom."

The nature of great bliss, too, is to be understood in the way of the perfections, as it is said in the Dhāraṇī of Entering Nonconceptuality:

"Utterly peaceful, unmoving, supreme,
Mahendra, equal to the unequaled;
therefore nonconceptual bliss
is ascertained by the bodhisattva."

Likewise, in the Meeting of the Father and the Son, too, having said, "Blessed One, there is a samādhi called 'pervading all phenomena with bliss.' Having attained that samādhi, the bodhisattva experiences only the feeling of bliss toward every object of focus, not suffering, nor what is neither suffering nor bliss, nor anything that is not bliss," and so forth, it says that even when they are cut and chopped into pieces the size of a kārṣāpaṇa coin and made into lumps, when they are cooked, and when they are trampled by elephants, only the perception of bliss arises.

These blisses, too, are not incompatible with the Mantra Vehicle. "Even non-bliss is supreme bliss," and "If it is accomplished, there is nothing else to accomplish": so says Sarahapāda.

Nor is this said only with regard to the method of this vehicle. For in the way of the perfections it is taught everywhere that the awakening mind alone, being the very nature of all the accumulations of merit and wisdom, is the chief cause of every excellence of worldly and transcendent bliss; and so it is in the way of mantra too. As it is said in the Source of the Radiant Vajra:

"Bliss is the fruit that merit bestows;
by blocking it, one is cut off from that aim.
Through merit one attains bliss,
and from bliss, too, one attains merit.
Because it is born from the awakening mind,
this merit is like space."

This very point is stated in the great yoga tantra, the glorious Vajraśekhara:

"Four kinds of persons in the world
do not succeed even through the rites,
not even through tens of millions of eons
of earnest application:
those who have not generated the mind of awakening,
and those without faith, do not succeed;
nor those with doubt and grasping,
nor those who do not act as instructed."

The enjoyment of the five sense pleasures also exists in the way of the perfections, as is clear from such words as "relying on desires and skilled in method." It should be considered that this is taught there in the manner of a rite as well. Thus it is said in the Heap of Jewels: "Kāśyapa, in a field of sugarcane, a field of rice, or a vineyard, heaps of manure become useful. In just the same way, the afflictions of bodhisattvas become useful."

The purification of objects is not absent there either, because the nature of the suchness of all phenomena is realized. Here, too, it is not that this is certainly present in every enjoyment of objects. Nor should it be said, "Was not everything, according to such words as 'Some, having abandoned objects,' said with the way of mantra alone in mind?", because no other method is taught.

The qualities that make up the fruit, such as the grounds, are no different in the two ways. What is said in the Ornament of the Vajra Essence, such as "Universal Light," are other names for the Very Joyful and the rest; for the terms "Very Joyful" and so on are also used, for the most part, in the way of mantra. Therefore, if there were no meditation on the generation stage, the way of mantra would have nothing to distinguish it.

Rather, from the very beginning, the nature of great bliss alone is to be discerned and cultivated through the way of mantra. And since that cannot be done by any other method, one is to cultivate it together with method. That method is precisely the generation stage, which is described as the tantra of method. That being so, one is to cultivate it endowed with seven limbs. Of these, the form of glorious Cakrasaṃvara is the limb of complete enjoyment. Embracing Vajravārāhī is the limb of union. Its joy is the limb of great bliss. Its being of one taste with emptiness is the limb of absence of intrinsic nature. Arising from that is the limb of compassion. Meditating on it without break is the limb of continuity. Not passing into nirvāṇa for as long as saṃsāra lasts is the limb of non-cessation. These are the seven limbs. The first three limbs are uncommon, for it is said that they are not taught in the other vehicles.

For that very reason, the Later Tantra of the Guhyasamāja teaches the generation stage, described as the tantra of method, as the basis, because it is the cause of the arising, abiding and increase of the completion stage. Therefore, abiding through meditation on the generation stage is not merely a limb for accomplishing worldly attainments; nor, taken by itself, is it merely a vessel for realizing suchness; nor does it actualize only that much. Enough, lest this run on too long.

Thus the conduct of this yogin of supreme meditation and recitation is taught in brief by the words "of the mind" and so forth; these too will be taught in detail later. This concludes the explanation of the thirteenth chapter.

To answer the twenty-second question and the others, "How are the maidens pleased?", the passage beginning "Then" is spoken. "Worship" means requesting the accomplishments one desires; and since that is preceded by worship, the topic here is the rite of worship. Some read it as "worship," which is easy to understand. Another purpose is stated with "outer" and so forth. "The yoginī" is the first case in the singular used in the sense of the second case; it also indicates fewness. "Not properly worshipped": this comes from the elision of the a of the negative particle nañ.

It says that for this, too, one should first perform the approach, with "one's own home" and so forth. The time of worship is stated with "of the dark" and so forth. "With ointment" means with the offering of fine scents. "One and" means one. Here "maiden" means a girl of up to ten years of age. "To be meditated upon" means in the form of Vajravārāhī and the others; and they are also to be worshipped. "Hero" means the yoginī. "The signs should be shown" means that the mudrā of welcome and the mudrā of homage should also be shown. Some say that, having placed the left hand on the crown of the head, one should bless her with her own mantra as appropriate. These points are taught in detail with "vajra" and so forth.

Here the procedure is as follows. On the tenth day of the dark or the bright fortnight, one sets out, as far as one is able, the requisites such as fish and meat, purifies them with the samaya pill, and fully accomplishes them with the rite for accomplishing nectar. Then, beginning with one maiden whose limbs are complete, and up to as many as twenty-four, as far as one is able, one calls them, in the form of goddesses, in the morning of the day. At dawn they are wiped, bathed and anointed, and invited, and their feet are washed with water of fine scents and flowers. In some secluded place one anoints, purifies and thoroughly perfumes the ground, and invites them to sit on comfortable seats in the order of the maṇḍala or in a row. Instantly, directly from emptiness, one meditates on them in the form of samaya beings within the celestial palace standing on Mount Meru, and causes the wisdom beings to enter them. Then one worships them properly with welcoming water, water for the feet, water for the mouth, eye ointment preceded by a purifying sprinkling, sindūra, fragrant powder and ointment, bouquets of fragrant flowers, incense and the like, and, if one has them, with garments, ornaments and so forth. One then sets before them requisites such as food and vegetables, like the offering food for the deities, and, preceded by the giving of torma, displays the lotus-turning gesture, embraces and the like. They are to be satisfied according to their wishes, and also with praises and songs.

The fruit of this special worship is shown with "here" and so forth; that is, worship is to be made to them. "At night" is the seventh case in the sense of the second. "Having worshipped": the worship is to be connected with "one should note the particular signs, pacifying and increasing." "Sneeze" means sneezes. "By which" means by the cause. "Laughter" in "the sound of fierce laughter" means laughing. "If it makes a sound" means if one laughs fiercely. "At the moment of that worship" means at whatever moment the worship is made. "Life itself, without doubt" means that the yogin will not live. "Worship" means if one worships. "In whatever way" means at whatever time. "Two" means two. "Of the practitioner" means by the practitioner. "Having done" means doing. "Pair and pair" means that they look at one another two by two, face to face and back to back. "Maiden" means the maiden alone; or else, it is what the maidens call "a maiden." "Says" means speaks. "Yoginī" means the yoginī in the form of a maiden turning her back and putting her fingernail in her mouth. "Again actions are needed" means it is fitting to give meat and so forth again; or else, that it is fitting, for the most part, to perform worship again. "Destruction by the harm of weapons and so forth" means destruction by the harm of weapons and the like. "By that" means there. "Laughing" means laughter. "Maiden" means the maidens. "Having cast away" means having scattered. "Directions" means in the four directions. "All agitated" means disparaging, that is, contorting. "Of me" means to me. "The act declines" means that the commitment that must be guarded has become exposed. "At eating" means at the time of eating. "When going" means going. "And again action is needed" means that actions such as worship are fitting for the yogin once again. The faults of the reverse are stated with "long" and so forth. "Fallen" means falling over and the like, or fighting with one another. This concludes the explanation of the fourteenth chapter.

Here, in the worship of maidens, in the feast gathering and elsewhere, a lotus vessel is to be used; this was not asked, or was asked only by implication, and the characteristics of the vessel are taught with "Then" and so forth. It should be understood the same way elsewhere too. Even vessels of clay and the like are lotus vessels, since they are made in the shape of a lotus vessel. "Brahmin" and the rest are not castes of humans but castes of skulls. These are taught with "white" and so forth: white is the brahmin caste; red and white is the kṣatriya caste; yellow and reddish yellow is the vaiśya caste; green is the śūdra caste. The irregular order is due to the meter. Colors such as ash are the low caste. "Inferior" means thin and badly shaped, and likewise "excessive." "Moist" means oily. Or else, the three words teach the inferior, the middling and the supreme. "Form" and the rest are easy to understand. "Examine thoroughly" means determine. "By the mantrin" means the mantrin. "Should accomplish" means should take up. "Destroys brahmins" means that fear of the yogin arises among brahmins; likewise "destroys cattle." "Mantrin inferior" means it leaves one without renown. "The mantra perishes" means the accomplishment of the mantra perishes. "Undeclined" means without decline. "Stream of vows" means continually generating, that is, generating moral discipline. "Endowed with fire" means facing the fire. "Thief" means it summons thieves. "Destroys women" means it ruins the women of the one who holds it. "Fierce one" means it destroys servants and the like. "Becomes a place" means one will not weary of going to the sacred places and the like. "Lord of the maṇḍala" means one should make many offerings. "The accomplishment of the practitioner" means it accomplishes what the practitioner desires; the intent is that it perfects whatever accomplishment one wishes to accomplish. "Fourth part" means the fourth piece. "Beggar" means it brings alms. "No fruit in accomplishing accomplishments" means that accomplishing the accomplishments of pacifying and the rest is fruitless; the intent is that it should be applied to fierce rites.

It says that the caste and so forth are also to be determined by the holes, with "holes" and so forth; these are to be known by pouring in water. "Lotus-like, marked by a lotus" means by its very round shape, like a lotus in full bloom, and by its color. "With pearls" means by its having a string of white dots like a string of pearls.

What is said is this: having examined the skulls of those who died after joining battle on a field of strife, or else of those cast away in great charnel grounds and the like, and having rejected those with faults, the yogin should without fail keep one that possesses the qualities as described. This is the explanation of the fifteenth chapter.

In order to answer the two questions beginning with the twenty-seventh, the passage beginning "the five nectars," the tantra spoke the passage beginning "Then." "Brahmin" means a brahmin woman. "Woman of the jewel family" means the jewel family, because the vajra too is itself a jewel. "Lotus woman" means one born of the lotus family. "That very dancer" means one thoroughly renowned in the world. "Tathāgata" means from the very family of Amitābha. "The first flower is highly praised": the intent is that if it is not available, another should be taken, even though it is not highly praised. The passage beginning "meat and" is the oral instruction that the others too are what is given by her: "Take just what has been taught."

What is said is this: brahmin women, caṇḍāla women, dancers, dyer women, and Caṇḍikā women, with limbs whitish-yellow, dark, reddish-yellow, green, and yellow, who possess the samaya: having first pleased them with worship and the rest, and ensuring that it does not fall within the reach of those who are not fit vessels, one should take Vairocana and the rest in the order taught and place them in a lotus vessel. Likewise the five, go, ku and the rest, not killed by anyone, as taught; if these are not to be had, one takes whatever is obtained. One mixes the ten substances with fragrant things such as musk, and on the tenth day makes them into pills the size of a pea, keeps them well hidden, and dries them in the shade. Then, in the midst of the feast gathering, one places them in the lotus vessel; setting the form of the sun on the right hand held upright and covering them with the form of the moon on the left hand, and preceded by deity yoga, one meditates on the assembly that accomplishes the nectar, and blesses it by reciting one hundred and eight times this, oṃ ha hoḥ hrīḥ, or else this, oṃ āḥ hūṃ. Then it is invariably to be applied in all rites. This is the explanation of the sixteenth chapter.

In order to answer the four questions beginning with the twenty-ninth, the passage beginning "Teach how one draws the maṇḍala oneself," the tantra spoke the passage beginning "Then." There, the verse and a half beginning "Thus" gives the characteristics of the preliminary approach and the protection wheel. The passage beginning "teaching" gives the characteristics of the master, since this is the occasion for them. From "temple" up to "the arising of the supreme saṃvara" is the purification of the ground; taking possession of the site and the rest are also explained by the word "purify." From "five" up to "by the rite as taught" is the laying of the lines. The remainder, beginning "the five jewels," is the laying of the colored powders; here this is explained by the very words "drawing the maṇḍala."

"Thus" means all these things that will be explained. "Some" means a little. "Ground" means the grounds, completely purified by having the very aspect of one's own mind as illusion-like. "Supplicate" means by the masters of the gates. "Together with a ḍākinī" means together with a wisdom partner.

"First, by the measure of four units" indicates the unit, the counting, and the division. "By this": a unit is a minor part, a quarter of the door. Therefore, on one side of the Brahmā line there is the line at the fourth minor part, for marking off the lotus pericarp. Outside that, "by this, eight units" is for marking off the lotus petals. "Again, by this, eight units" is the root line, on both sides of the Brahmā line, at an interval of four minor parts outside the eight minor parts. From here on, all the lines are to be understood as on both sides. "From that, also by two units" is the line of the wall, at an interval of one minor part. "From that, by this, four units" is the line of the ledge, at an interval of two minor parts. "Again, by this, two units" is the line of the jewel band, at the end of one minor part. "From that, by this, four units" is the line of the nets and half-nets, at an interval of two minor parts. "From that, by this, two units": two lines are to be laid, at intervals of one minor part each, for the jewel fringe and the parapet.

If one asks how those stated as eight minor parts and so on become four minor parts and so on, it is stated with "Half of that" and the rest: half of what was stated is on the two sides, and the two sides are to be drawn, because on both sides the lines and the rest, being equal in form, stand as if joined face to face. By these is taught the celestial palace together with its retinue, extending to the parapet. These are only a brief presentation; the complete account is to be learned from other tantras.

Those very things are stated with "measuring cord" and the rest. The mark of the measuring cord is what "marked by this" refers to. The mark of the measuring cord is the minor part, and these are sixty-four. Because the case ending has been dropped, "maṇḍala" means "of the maṇḍala." If one asks to what extent the maṇḍala has sixty-four minor parts, it is stated with "all around the wheel" and the rest; the circumference of the wheel is the vajra garland. "By the rite" means in the sequence of division into the doors, the door recesses, the jambs, the side walls, the archways, and the rest. If one asks how, it is stated: "just as it is."

Or else, if one asks what is to be done once the Brahmā lines, the diagonal lines and the root lines are in place, it is stated with "first" and the rest, as before. "Again" means as far as the jewel band. "From that" is for the band of nets and half-nets. "From that, four units" means as far as the band of the jewel fringe and half of the archway. For the maṇḍala of sixty-four minor parts, the archway too rises four minor parts. "Again" is for marking off the archway. "From that" is for the wheel and its rim, of two minor parts. "From that" means that two lines are to be laid, at intervals of two minor parts each, for the garland of petals, the vajra garland, and the garland of light rays.

If one asks whether its garland of petals and its vajra garland are also two minor parts each: that is the place of the second minor part beyond the line at the edge of the rim. "To be drawn half on the two sides" means that just as the half has come about, so it is to be drawn on both sides. Therefore the places of the petals and of the vajra garland come to one minor part each. Moreover, because the Blessed One resides within the celestial palace encircled by the eight charnel grounds, the charnel grounds too are to be drawn; this is what is stated in the passage beginning "vajra."

Now, this is the outer meaning. The master who wishes to draw the maṇḍala, in order to be free of obstructors, blesses himself with the nectar pill, and, preceded by the giving of torma, meditates intensely on himself as the wheel of deities by the rite taught in the chapter on the deities, and recites its mantra a number of times, such as a hundred thousand, until he is able to imagine vividly, all at once within himself, the emanating and gathering of the form of the Lord of the Wheel together with the wheel, complete in all its aspects, and until he obtains its permission. This is the rite of the preliminary approach.

Then, in some pleasing place, for the sake of drawing the maṇḍala, in the evening one makes a maṇḍala of one's chosen deity with fragrances and the like and worships it properly. Having given torma, one places them in the sky, and having satisfied with tormas the field protectors and others who dwell there, one says three times:

Whatever gods and nāgas dwell here,
yakṣas, rākṣasas, or others too,
this ground for the sake of a maṇḍala
I request of you: please grant it to me.

When they have granted it in a dream or the like, or else when one knows that they have not forbidden it, on the morning of the next day one supplicates the Blessed One with his retinue abiding in the sky, and imagines in one's mind that he has given his permission accordingly. At midday, by meditating on the protection wheel, one subdues the obstructors, recites the mantra for all rites, and scatters white mustard seed, water and ash on the ground as a purifying offering. There, one imagines the form of the earth goddess Pṛthivī, seated in vajra posture, as will be explained, and, preceded by water for the feet and the rest, supplicates her three times with "Come here, come here" and so forth. She says "Do so," and one sees her rise into the sky. One then knows: "Now it is ground free of obstructors." Having given torma, one determines the earth-lord nāga, and where digging and the rest are possible, one should do the digging and the rest.

The examination of the earth lord is this. On the ground of the maṇḍala one makes a square diagram twice the size of the maṇḍala and divides the whole into ninety squares. There the earth-lord nāga, through the three months beginning with Bhādrapada, Mārgaśīrṣa, Phālguna, Jyeṣṭha and so forth, lies in turn with his head toward the east, the south and so on, facing south and so on, his body covering nine squares. Through the ninety days of Bhādrapada and the rest, on each day he presses one square; one examines his movement also by dividing the watches of the day that have passed. Avoiding entirely twenty or twenty-one squares on the side of the head, and also two or three squares at the belly, and having determined the back and the tail as well, one should dig at the belly. According to the division of lesser, middling and supreme accomplishment, one digs as deep as the knees, the hips or the navel. This is the digging of the ground.

Then, having removed the impurities of the soil, one fills the pit with that same earth mixed with fragrant water; if it does not fill, then with other earth as well. One sprinkles it with fragrant water over which the mantra for all rites has been recited, and pounds it, making it half a cubit high, like the back of a tortoise, slightly lower toward the northeast. In a mansion and the like there is no digging and the rest at all. Touching it with the hand and reciting the syllable hūṃ, one performs the purifying scattering with ash and the rest as before; then, beginning from the east, one smears it well with the five products of the cow and sprinkles it with scented water.

So, ground purified by digging and the rest, or any other, is suitable. Saying oṃ bhū khaṃ, one makes it into space, and with this, hūṃ laṃ hūṃ, one meditates on it as having the nature of vajra. With these three mantras, oṃ medini vajrī bhāva vajra bandha hūṃ, oṃ hana hana vajra krodha hūṃ phaṭ, and oṃ āḥ hūṃ śodhaya śodhaya rakṣa rakṣa hūṃ phaṭ, one touches it with the hand and blesses it, and recites the mantra for all rites. On the ground, in which the five jewels, the five medicines, the five grains and the five essences have been hidden, and which has been smeared with scented water, preceded by torma and the guest-water offering, one meditates that the earth goddess abiding in the sky has entered within it, recites oṃ āḥ hūṃ, and, preceded by the lotus-turning gesture, recites the hundred-syllable mantra. This is the purification of the ground.

Then, seated in the center of the maṇḍala ground strewn with flowers, the one holding vajra and bell, endowed with the yoga of the maṇḍala that is to be drawn, should perform the protection wheel and the rest. Having vanquished the obstructors, he holds the incense burner and, with a vajra tongue born from hūṃ, says:

Buddhas dwelling in all directions without exception,
please attend to me.
I, the vajra-holder named so-and-so,
will draw a maṇḍala here.
Buddhas and all the rest, come,
and here bestow the accomplishments in full.

Thus he invites the perfect buddhas and the rest and worships them with offerings. To them, abiding in the sky and so on, the disciples endowed with that yoga, holding vajra and vajra-bell and standing in the four directions, say:

Peace of all the Tathāgatas,
abode of all the Tathāgatas,
supreme among all selfless phenomena:
please teach the sublime maṇḍala.
Perfectly complete in every characteristic,
having abandoned all that are not characteristics,
Samantabhadra, supreme of body:
please teach the sublime maṇḍala.
Arisen from the supreme Dharma, from peace,
purifying the conduct of primordial wisdom,
Samantabhadra, supreme of speech:
please teach the sublime maṇḍala.
Great mind of all sentient beings,
pure by nature and without stain,
Samantabhadra, supreme of conduct:
please guide us and teach the maṇḍala.

With these four verses they should supplicate him, and if they are not actually present, it may be done by imagining them. Abiding thus, he takes the wheel of deities that he has meditated on, preceded by the emanation of the maṇḍala wheel and the rest: with two vajra fists, index fingers extended and their tips touching, facing downward, he places it at the crown of his head, says hūṃ vajrod tiṣṭha, and raises it into the sky. Then he himself stands up, faces east, stands with feet even, and pays homage to the perfect buddhas with palms joined at the crown of his head. In an instant he should take on the body of the wrathful Vajrahūṃkāra, with frowning brows, bared fangs and a haughty bearing. In the right and left eyes, from the syllables ma and ḍa, come the forms of the sun and moon, and with the syllable hūṃ he makes the two pupils like the fire at the end of the eon. He looks in all directions with the vajra gaze and holds the pride:

I myself am the vajra-holder;
Vajrasattva himself am I;
I am the Buddha, the great king;
I am the vajra-holder of great strength;
I am the lord of yogins, the king;
I am the steadfast Vajrapāṇi.

Then, meditating on blazing vajras at the soles of his feet, with a haughty bearing, preceded by the lotus-turning gesture, brandishing the vajra and ringing the bell, he goes with vajra steps to the northeast. Beginning from there, moving clockwise, he dances as well as he can with the vajra steps and the rest, and says three times "Go elsewhere!" and so forth. Having subdued the obstructors through the infinite wrathful ones he emanates, he blesses the maṇḍala ground, down to the realm below the earth, as having the nature of vajra. Then he should dance as he is able and as he wishes. If he is unable or does not wish to, there is no fault even if he does not dance. This is the taking possession of the ground.

Then he reflects, "In order that beings may attain the state of Vajradhara, I shall make it free of obstructors." In an instant, immediately after emptiness, there is a sun born from raṃ; on it a crossed vajra born from hūṃ; on that a hūṃ; and from the vajras born from the light rays of that hūṃ he generates the ground, the fence, the tent, the canopy and the net of arrows, all of vajra nature. In an instant Vajrasattva is transformed, and he becomes Vajrahūṃkāra, standing on a variegated lotus and a sun, trampling Bhairava and Kālarātri. He has three faces, the central, right and left, blue, yellow and green, each face with three eyes. He wears the six bone ornaments, a garland of human heads and a skirt of tiger skin, and is adorned with the eight nāgas. With his hands holding vajra and bell he forms two vajra fists, joined back to back, the little fingers hooked like a chain and the index fingers fully extended; with this mudrā of Trailokyavijaya he embraces the wisdom consort who is his own light. His right hands hold a hook and a noose, his left hands a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga. His body is solely black. With a terrifying nature he gazes over all the three worlds. With the light rays of the syllable hūṃ at his heart he summons the obstructors of the ten directions and hands them over to the ten wrathful executioners born from it.

The pegs are eight finger-widths in length, and in thickness a third of the length in finger-widths. They are made of khadira wood, iron or bone. One puts them in a new vessel, anoints them with white mustard and red sandalwood, wraps them with threads of the five colors, and worships them. The ten pegs, bound with garlands of red flowers, one visualizes as having a single point below the navel and, above it, the forms of the wrathful ones of their respective directions. The yogin holds them in his left hand, and in his right holds a hammer that is a transformation of the principal deity's emblem; by the rite taught for the protection wheel he drives the pegs outside the house of the maṇḍala and so protects it. At each session one should worship them individually with flowers and the like. This is the rite of driving the pegs.

Then the ground is smeared with fragrances, the five nectars, and substances over which the three syllables have been recited, and strewn with flowers. The master, having bathed and adorned himself with clothing and the like, whatever is to be had, and wearing a headdress of red cloth over which oṃ āḥ hūṃ svāhā and oṃ vajra uṣṇīṣa hūṃ phaṭ have been recited seven times, sits in the center facing east. Endowed with the yoga of the Lord of the Wheel, he meditates on his own samaya wheel, exactly as it is, in the maṇḍalas made of nectar, sandalwood and the like at the places of the deities. He causes the wisdom wheel to enter, and also performs offering, praise and the tasting of nectar.

Outside the area meditated upon, located in the east and the other directions, for the sake of pacifying, enriching and all rites, the vases are made: the belly round, sixteen finger-widths, and fourteen finger-widths in height; above it the neck, six finger-widths; the width of the mouth from the edge of the lip to the edge of the lip, eight finger-widths; the opening of the neck, four finger-widths; on each side of the neck opening, straight across, the breadth of the lip two finger-widths, and the overhanging lip two finger-widths; taken together from beneath the belly to the rim of the mouth, twenty finger-widths in height. They are made of clay, copper and the like, not black and without cracks, in a shape that accords with the rite.

Having emptied them, one meditates on them through the syllable vaṃ as forms of jewel, and draws the emblems of the deities. One puts inside fragrant water, nectar, and the five jewels, the five medicines and the five grains; adorns the mouths with branches; binds the necks with a pair of cloths together with garlands; and winds them with white threads over which hūṃ has been recited seven times, while proclaiming oṃ vajra śikhara ruṭa maṭa diśāṃ bandha bandha hūṃ. Under the canopy, in the order in which the emblems are set out on the colored-powder maṇḍala, as will be explained, one places them one by one upon suitable seats on maṇḍalas of fragrance, and consecrates them.

The drawing of the emblems is as follows. On the principal deity's Victorious Vase, upon a source of phenomena, a variegated lotus and a sun, is the emblem of the principal deity, a black five-pronged vajra. On the other vases, upon a variegated lotus and a sun, is a curved knife. Pearl, gold, ruby, coral and sapphire are the five jewels. Bṛhatī, kaṇṭakārī, white and red daṇḍotpala, and aparājitā are the five medicines. Sesame, barley, peas, rice and wheat are the five grains. Mango, nāgakesara, aśoka, aśvattha and udumbara, or others too, are the branches. The vases are five, for the individual deities, or else only for those who dwell in the place of the heart, and in addition the vase for all rites. If these are lacking, the principal deity's vase alone will do; in that case the emblem of the vase for all rites is placed on the left side of the principal deity's emblem.

Here is the sequence of consecration. First, above the Victorious Vase, one recites these seven times: oṃ vajra amṛte udakaḥ ṭhaḥ hūṃ and oṃ āḥ hūṃ. One sets down the lotus vessel, holding the five nectars, whatever are to be had, made into wisdom nectar, having the nature of vajra atoms, together with fragrant water and consecrated with hūṃ, along with a vajra tied with a garland of flowers. Inside the vase, in the water summoned with oṃ taphe taphe svāhā, water having the nature of the water that flows from the maṇḍala of the deities, one instantly visualizes the wheel of deities together with its support. Seeing the wisdom wheel summoned by the light rays of the seed syllable at one's heart, and preceded by water for the feet and the rest, one causes it to enter there. It too melts through great passion and becomes of one taste with the water poured in before. On the emblem too one visualizes the samaya deity and the wisdom deity becoming one. Holding the end of the flower garland tied to the vajra with the left fist, with the mantra of hūṃ, one recites a hundred times together with the eight essence mantras of the Lord of the Wheel, and casts into the water flowers empowered with the mantra of Khaṇḍarohā. So too for the other vases; the empowering of the water is done with each one's own mantra.

Then one worships them properly with flower garlands and the like, purifies them with the water of the vase for all rites, and says: "As long as the activities of the maṇḍala and the rest are not complete, turn back the obstructors in this vase!" One offers torma, touches them, praises them with the eight-line mantra, and rings the bell. Taking a little water from them, one pours it into a skull cup and places it on a stand on the north side of the east door, for the purifying sprinkling of oneself and the disciples. The worship of the vases, the mantras and the rest are to be done each day. This is the rite of consecrating the vases.

Then, sitting in the center of the ground and making a maṇḍala, one meditates on the earth goddess Pṛthivī arisen from vaṃ. Imagining three hūṃ syllables on a moon on the right hand, one strikes with the tips of three fingers. Urged by that sound and by the light rays of the mantra, the form of the wisdom being emerges and becomes one with the earth goddess. Her mind is joined with joy; she is yellow and peaceful, clad in white, endowed with various ornaments; with her two hands, the left and the other, she holds a fine vase of gold and makes the gesture of fearlessness. She is one-faced and seated in front. One worships her properly with guest water and the rest, and respectfully says three times:

oṃ Come here, come here, great goddess
of the earth, mother of the world,
filled entirely with the nature of jewels,
adorned with divine ornaments,
with necklaces and anklets that jingle,
you who make offerings to Vajrasattva:
accepting this guest-water offering,
accomplish well the work of the maṇḍala.
hī hi hi hi hāṃ svāhā

So one invites her, requests her to remain near and within, and consecrates. She says, "I will do so," and one imagines her dissolving into the ground. Turning the vajra-binding gesture downward, one presses the ground with it and empowers it three times with oṃ vajrī bhava hūṃ, meditating that the ground, down to the realm below the earth, is made vajra by the masses of blazing hūṃ syllables streaming forth from the vajra binding. Saying oṃ vajra driḍha tiṣṭha three times, one blesses it. Thus the ground becomes one's own. This is the rite of consecrating Vasundharā.

Then, sitting to the east of that ground, which has been smeared with fragrances and the like and strewn with flowers, and facing west, one makes with fragrances mixed with the five nectars a square maṇḍala in the center, at the place of the five, the principal deity and the rest, and round maṇḍalas for the others. There one invites the Blessed One, summoned by the light rays of the seed syllables at their respective hearts, worships properly, preceded by guest water and the rest, offers praise, and tastes the nectar. Then, planting the right knee on the ground, with a tongue that is a single-pointed vajra born from hūṃ and blessed by it, holding the incense burner in the right hand and ringing the bell with the left, one says:

Blessed One Cakrasaṃvara, supreme vajra,
king of awareness, I bow and praise you.
Protector whose very nature is compassion,
I wish to draw the maṇḍala.
Out of love for sentient beings,
and also to make offering to you,
it is fitting, O Blessed One,
that you show your kindness to me, devoted.
Attend to me, you buddhas,
and grant the purpose of the wheel of beings.
Bodhisattvas, those abiding in the fruit,
and whatever other mantra deities there are,
gods and world protectors,
bhūtas, those abiding in perfect awakening,
those who delight in the teaching for beings,
and whoever possess the divine eye:
I, so-and-so, Great Vajra,
shall draw the maṇḍala of the arising of Cakrasaṃvara,
with its surroundings, as far as I am able,
in order to purify beings.
Therefore, embracing me and my disciples
with loving care,
it is fitting that all of you, together with the maṇḍala,
remain close by.

One says this three times, fixing its meaning in mind, and so supplicates. This is the rite of consecrating the deities.

Then, having properly worshipped the consecrated wheel of deities, one places it in the sky above the maṇḍala ground for the sake of the wisdom lines and the rest. With disciples one has empowered oneself, and only such, five in number, divided into the four masters of the gates and one karmavajra, or if these are lacking, even with one, one faces east, endowed with the yoga of the lord of the maṇḍala. One places threads spun by a maiden, five by five, in five vessels, and colors each set separately white, yellow, red, green and black, mixed with good fragrances and colors, white mustard and the five nectars. One arranges them in order on the fingertips of the left hand, up to the thumb.

One sees the five tathāgatas, complete, born from bhūṃ oṃ hrīḥ hā hūṃ and abiding in the sky, into whom the tathāgatas of their respective directions have gathered, and sees in the hearts of the five tathāgatas the threads of primordial wisdom, having the nature of light rays born from bhrūṃ and the rest. With this, oṃ āḥ śāśvata vajra sūtraṃ me prayaccha mahāmaṇḍal sūtraṇāya hūṃ, substituting the appropriate name, one supplicates Ratnasambhava and the others too. One's two eyes are a moon and a yellow sun born from jaḥ. Saying oṃ dīpta dṛṣṭa aṃkuśi jāḥ, with eyes moving very swiftly and fluttering the lashes, one urges Vairocana and the rest with the hook-like look of blazing eyes, and causes the wisdom threads to enter, just as they are, into the threads in one's own hand. Then, in the form of Khaṇḍarohā, together with the disciple, saying oṃ anonya anugatāḥ sarva dharmāḥ sphara sphara anuphrapiṣṭāḥ sarva dharmā atyanta anupraviṣṭāḥ sarva dharmā oṃ āḥ hūṃ, one first twists each set of five threads firmly, and then twists the five sets into one.

Apart from the extra portion pressed down by the hands of the guru and the disciple, its length should be twice the maṇḍala, and its thickness a twentieth of the door, as appropriate. After that, one puts it on the earlier ground in a clean vessel smeared with fragrance, with fine fragrances and the like, and worships it with flowers and the rest endowed with the three syllables. Pressing it with the hand, one protects it with Khaṇḍarohā. In the right and the other eye one sees a sun and a moon born from mā and ṭa, and as before makes them firm with vajra driṣṭi maṭa. One sprinkles it with nectar water over which the Khaṇḍarohā mantra has been recited, touches it with the right hand holding the vajra, and recites this: oṃ vajra samaya sūtra mātikrama hūṃ. Then one empowers it one hundred and eight times with the three syllables together with the eight, and consecrates it.

Then, with the mantra oṃ sarva saṃśodhani hūṃ phaṭ and with the water of all the vases, one performs the purifying sprinkling on the ground, in the division of directions where the five maṇḍalas were made. One looks upon the maṇḍala that is to be drawn in the sky above the ground smeared with fragrance and strewn outside with flowers, worships it properly, preceded by guest water and the rest, and prostrates. Oneself endowed with its yoga, one sees the disciple in the form of the deity of all rites. One imagines in the left and the other eye of oneself and of him a yellow moon and sun born from jaḥ. With the vajra steps, one holds at the navel the cord gripped in the left fist, and the disciple does likewise. Urging the cord and its jaḥ syllables with three jaḥ syllables, the disciple too with three jaḥ syllables, drawn by the jaḥ syllables, they stand facing each other: standing in the west and looking east, and standing in the south and looking north, they lay the two Brahmā lines in the air, level with the navel. Saying oṃ vajra samaya sūtra mātikrama hūṃ hūṃ oṃ svā āḥ h, one holds the cord with the thumb and fingers of the right hand that holds the vajra, and, urging with the sound of cymbals, meditates that the tathāgatas abiding in the sky dissolve into it, and sounds the cymbals.

Then, with the hands of the guru and the disciple, their left fists pressing the cord evenly, they lay on the ground, as in the sky, the two Brahmā lines, reaching as far as the garland of light rays. Standing at Agni's corner and at Nairṛti's corner, they lay the two lines toward Vāyu's corner and Īśāna's corner, as far as the line of the parapet. Then, standing at Agni's corner and looking north, they lay the root line on the east; standing at Vāyu's corner and looking south, on the west; standing at that same place and looking east, on the north; and again standing at Agni's corner and looking west, on the south. Thus the four root lines are to be laid to the measure of the wheel. Circumambulation is everywhere clockwise, through intense aspiration to the equality of all phenomena.

As for sitting down: the master sits with the left leg in the half cross-legged posture and the right foot planted on the ground; the disciple does the reverse. The line is held in the left hand in a vajra fist, and one moves along behind it; and it should be understood that the intense visualization of the line in space likewise applies to every laying of lines.

Then, at the eight portions of the wheel, four small units, two on each side of the Brahmā line, are to be erased. With the intense conviction, "I shall remove the māras that have become obstacles to the body of the Blessed One and to the doors of liberation," one generates, with the syllable hīḥ, the left eye and the other as moon and sun, looks all around with a wrathful gaze, says hī hī, and erases them.

Outward from the root line, with intervals of one, two, one,
two, one and one,
six lines run straight across from the corners,
successively longer, starting from the Brahmā line.
The first two lines, of three small units,
touch the tip of the second small unit.
With an interval of seven small units,
three straight lines rise from the plinth line;
in length they are five small units,
each leaving one small unit between.
Where they meet the tips of the straight lines,
those are the first lines.
The line projecting from the tip of the root line,
four small units, and two small units
straight above; and straight across from that
it is joined at the eighth small unit.
Above the eighth small unit is the beam;
know it to be at the tenth small unit.
Then, straight across for the archway,
four small units above the three doors:
eight and eight and twelve,
twenty-four, eight and twenty-four,
twelve, eight and twelve,
twenty-four and eight, in order,
there, above the portion of the door:
thus there are eleven bands.
Straight across at the side of the Brahmā line,
four and a half small units at the end;
a band of two and a half small units;
the lines are three and a half, and three.
From five small units, the second,
and the third too, are three small units;
from four small units, the fourth is three;
at three and a half small units, five;
three and a half; from three, six;
from those five small units, three;
eight small units, seven parts, one;
and at the end, the sixth small unit.
One outside and one inside;
the cord reaches right to the tip.
Again it bends inward
and rests at the end of half a small unit.
Above the archway is the wheel,
adorned with deer, two small units;
above the wheel, likewise, the horns;
and likewise four circles:
the first from the tips of the horns, the rest
each one small unit apart.

The lines of the circles are drawn as follows. At the center of the maṇḍala one visualizes a peg of khadira wood, five-pointed above and single-pointed below, arisen from hūṃ; one recites the peg-planting mantra one hundred and eight times and strikes it with a vajra hammer to the striking mantra; then the circles are drawn with the cord made of twice five strands, as described above, consecrated with mantra.

Then, having properly worshipped the cord with offerings preceded by a torma, one says oṃ āḥ hūṃ vajra muḥ, and the wisdom lines depart and are placed in the hearts of the tathāgatas dwelling in the sky; the threads of the five colors should be left in place just as they are. This is the rite of laying the lines.

Then, having offered torma, one takes the colors made of rice and the like, arisen from baṃ laṃ raṃ haṃ yaṃ: the white, yellow, red, green and black colors, each produced in turn from its own seed, which are of the nature of Vairocana and the others. Into them the wisdom colors are invited and absorbed by the rite taught for the merging of the lines, so that they become of one taste. Over these, on which the seeds of Vairocana and the others have been recited, one recites oṃ vajra citra samaya hūṃ one hundred and eight times. Then, as before, one generates splendor with a blazing gaze and places a hūṃ on each. Over the many colored powders one says, "By the truth whereby sentient beings and the buddhas, the blessed ones, are attached to sentient beings and attached in return, by that truth may the colored powders blaze!" Having thus set them ablaze by the blessing of truth, and having worshipped them with flowers and so on, one should place them in new vessels.

Then the peg standing at the center of the maṇḍala is drawn out with the four-hūṃ mantra that will be explained. Having mentally visualized it as entering the vajra in one's own hand, one fills the peg's hole completely with the five colored powders and levels it. With the sound of the bell and with vajra songs one dispels bad omens, saying:

This is the pure dharmadhātu,
which fully liberates the realm of sentient beings.
The king of vajra holders himself,
the abode of all tathāgatas,
completely free from every fault,
truly abides within the wheel.

With this, one intensely conceives it as the completely pure dharmadhātu. First, sitting within the maṇḍala, with the right hand holding a vajra and the left in a vajra fist, one says oṃ vajra citra samaya hūṃ, faces east, and, beginning from Īśāna's corner and going clockwise, lays down just a little of the powder of the five colors. "Afterwards, by another or by oneself" means whichever is convenient. The strokes should not be thick, thin, broken, crooked or mingled with one another; all are one twentieth of the door. When entering and leaving, too, one mentally lifts the strokes of colored powder into the sky and says oṃ vajra begākrama hūṃ, in order to pacify the fault of stepping over the lines and so on.

Here, first: at the hub of the crossed vajra, on a yellow sun upon the anthers of a multicolored lotus, is the Blessed One's fierce five-pronged vajra, blue. On the red petals of the east, north, west and south are the curved knives of Ḍākinī and the others. The petals of the intermediate directions, Agni's, Nairṛti's and so on, are yellow, green, yellow, and black like a rain cloud. Outside the petals, within the root lines, the grounds of the east and the other directions, going counterclockwise, are the colors of Vairocana, Amoghasiddhi, Amitābha and Ratnasambhava. In the doors of the east and so on are the curved knives of Kākāsyā and the others, and in the corners of Agni and so on those of Yamadāḍhī and the others, arranged counterclockwise and clockwise respectively, resting on multicolored lotuses and suns. On the two sides of the doors are eight vases. Outside the root lines come the bands of colored powder: beginning from the inside, the strokes are black, green, red, yellow and white. The plinth outside that is made in whatever way is beautiful. At the junctures of the doors and the door recesses are jewels resting on crescent moons, and above them half vajras.

The space within the curve of the archway and the pillars, too, are each the color of the tathāgata of their own direction. The pillars stand on vases set on square plinths, and are bound with jewels at the base and at the top. On the band of hanging silk are jeweled necklaces hanging from the mouths of lions above elephants. On the jewel band, at the corners, are triangles, squares, drops and circles in turn, studded with jewels. On the net band are nets of pearls fastened to four golden girdles and strung on red thread issuing from the mouths of makaras, with two clusters of gems bound in gold in the middle. On the sides of the nets are half nets, their tips made beautiful by large gems set in gold. In some places, between the bells, the two sides are adorned with yak-tail whisks and with jewel garlands a little shorter. In some places the two sides are adorned with garlands of flowers. Within the nets are jewels marked with a lotus, a crescent moon and a half vajra; in some places a sun disk; in some places a vajra resting on a lotus. The bakulis are white. The parapets are white, in the shape of half-opened lotus petals. Above the door walls, on the beam band, is the beam, with leaf ornaments of various jewels. Above that is the sky band, blue.

On the parapets, in the eight directions, are victory banners and flags standing on vases of gold or any other material: they bear a crescent moon, a jewel and a vajra, with single-tongued and double-tongued streamers hanging from bells tied to the tips of jeweled staffs, and are marked at their tips with the king of beasts, the king of geese, a peacock, a fish and a makara. The flags are the same, but without emblems. At the corners are umbrellas.

On the eleven bands of the archway, beginning from the tops of the pillars, come in order: a bakuli, jewels, horse hooves, gold, makaras, gold again, horse hooves, jewels, horse hooves, gold, and parapets. Resting on the parapet band, on lotus and moon seats, are golden wheels. On the two sides of each wheel are a buck and a doe made of gold, facing the wheel, lying with necks raised, in whatever way is beautiful. Above the wheel are the horns, a crossed vajra of two small units, the color of the tathāgata of its own direction. The curving horns stand on the two sides of the archway; nearer than these, on the parapets, are umbrellas held by the handle by monkeys at the side of the doe's tail, in whatever way is beautiful. At and near the corners of the palace are wish-fulfilling trees.

The multicolored lotus has green anthers, yellow filaments and petals of various colors; its ground is white and red. The line of the vajra garland is black, with three-pronged or five-pronged vajras; its ground is made in whatever way is beautiful. The light rays are of five colors. Outside that the charnel grounds are to be drawn, but only if no unfortunate persons are gathered, and not otherwise. This too should be learned from the tantra alone.

Then one smears the outside of the maṇḍala with urine together with the five nectars, recited over with the all-purpose mantra and with the syllable hūṃ; reciting that, one sprinkles the wheel with water to purify it, and properly worships the remaining colored powders. Saying oṃ āḥ hūṃ vajra muḥ, the wisdom powders depart, and those remaining in the vessels should be set aside in a secluded place. This is the rite of laying the colored powders.

Then the victory vase, which was resting there beforehand, is carried clockwise around the wheel of the maṇḍala and placed on the ground outside the garland of light rays, or, if there are charnel grounds, outside them, slightly to the right of the line of the maṇḍala's east door. To its left go the vase of Ḍākinī and the all-purpose vase, proceeding clockwise; to the right, that of Kākāsyā. To the left of the north door goes that of Lāmā, and to the right that of Ulūkāsyā. To the left of the west door goes that of Khaṇḍarohā, and to the right that of Śvānāsyā. To the left of the south door goes that of Rūpiṇī, and to the right that of Sūkarāsyā. In the intermediate directions, Agni's and the others, are the vases of Yamadāḍhī and the others. On the four corner vases are four arrows fletched with vulture feathers. They are to be placed with the mantra oṃ vajra yakṣa hūṃ. This is the rite of arranging the vases.

After that, the canopy, umbrella, yak-tail whisks and so on are charged with the syllable hūṃ and multiplied with the mantra oṃ vajra sbaraṇa khaṃ, and with them one makes offerings as far as one is able and in whatever way is beautiful. The master then bathes and so forth, sprinkles the maṇḍala to purify it with water over which the all-purpose mantra has been recited, and protects it with that same mantra. Sitting to the east of the maṇḍala, facing west, holding vajra and bell, he makes the drawn maṇḍala, whose nature is the Lord of the Wheel (Cakreśvara), empty in an instant. He then meditates on all the rites in full: the protection wheel, the striking of the obstructors with pegs, and so on, and resolves it to be the very nature of the maṇḍala. Together with a fully accomplished outer consort (mudrā) or a wisdom consort, he invites the wisdom wheel with sounds of intense joy, properly worships it with water for the guests and so on, and draws it inside. It is melted by great passion, sent out through the vajra path into the lotus, and emanated as the wisdom wheel; he contemplates them as fully seated in the pledge wheel, each in its place. Immediately after the eyes and so on have been blessed, the tathāgatas, invited by the light rays of the seed in his own heart, should confer empowerment and set the seal.

Then, having made offerings with the emanated goddesses, he worships fully, as far as he is able, with a pair of śītalika cloths and so on, and with foods to eat and to chew and so on; the outer yoginīs make offerings with dance and so on. Ringing the bell, he praises with the eight-line mantra hymn, and then places the flower of his own wisdom consort and the awakening mind in a lotus vessel, makes them into nectar, and offers it.

Satisfied by tasting that nectar, one should intensely conceive oneself as having the nature of great bliss. One recites the mantra of the Lord of the Wheel one hundred and eight times, those of the others twenty-one times each, and also the hundred-syllable mantra, and then offers the torma for all the bhūtas by the rite of the torma chapter that will be explained.

After that, one turns the right hand palm upward, bends the middle and ring fingers a little, and holds it before the mouth, with its elbow between the knees. Squatting and keeping silent, one strokes the face many times with the fingertips, and three times touches the ears, the eyes, the nose, the mouth, the hands, the navel and the crown. This is the ritual touching.

Then, endowed with the yoga of Trailokyavijaya, one holds a garland of flowers and recites the mantra of the four-faced one beginning oṃ sumbha. Entering the maṇḍala with vajra impetus, twirling the vajra and ringing the bell, one makes three clockwise circuits with vajra steps, while the masters of the east, south and other doors recite in turn the four mantras oṃ hūṃ phaṭ, oṃ hūṃ āḥ phaṭ, oṃ hūṃ āḥ phaṭ, and oṃ hūṃ hana māraya duṣṭān ghātaya ghātaya āḥ āḥ hūṃ hrīḥ phaṭ. One offers the garland to the Blessed One and stands at the west, north and other doors; seeing the east, south and other doors opened by the mantra oṃ vajra utbhaṭaya samaya praveśaya hūṃ, one says oṃ samayaṃ praviśāyami. Dancing once more, one takes the garland, binds it on one's own crown, and bows to the wheel, praying, "Please grant accomplishments to all sentient beings!" Then one practices meditative concentration and recitation. Flags too should be offered, as far as one is able, to the deities of the maṇḍala and to the lords of the directions. This is the rite of accomplishing the maṇḍala.

In the maṇḍala thus completed one performs consecration, empowerment, fire offering and so on, and also self-empowerment; then, having first held a feast gathering, one should dismiss the maṇḍala. The dismissal is this. Outside, one makes torma offerings, worship and so on for the guardians of the directions. Entering inside, one bows to the wheel, presents water for the guests and so on to the Lord of the Wheel, fully dedicates the virtue, and makes three circuits. Sitting at the east door facing the Blessed One, one recites the hundred-syllable mantra and asks forbearance with this verse:

Whatever, through lack of ability, O Lord,
through not obtaining and not knowing,
I have done deficient or in excess,
it is fitting that you forgive it all.
Whatever mantra has lapsed, whatever act has lapsed,
whatever part of the rite has lapsed,
it is fitting that the hero forgive;
Supreme Lord, be gracious!

Then with oṃ vajra muḥ and the rest, and also with oṃ āḥ hūṃ, the wisdom wheel departs, and one meditates that the wheel one has emanated is within oneself. Saying oṃ akāro mukhaṃ sarvadharmāṇāṃ ādyanutpannatvāt oṃ āḥ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā and oṃ laḥ hūṃ muḥ, one intensely conceives all phenomena as unborn. Ringing the bell, with the vajra in one's hand one wipes away the powder, beginning from the east door and following the path of the Brahmā line as far as the place of the principal deity. One places a little of the colored powder on one's own crown, and then the lotus and the rest should be erased. Then the doors and corners should be demolished with the vajra in one's hand.

After that, the pegs are drawn out with the four-hūṃ mantra, oṃ vajra kīlaya utkīlaya sarvakīlan vajradhara ājñāya hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ hoḥ, and bathed with milk over which oṃ ruru sphuru jvala taṣṭhi siddhalocani sarva artha sādhani svāhā has been recited one hundred and eight times; the deities of the pegs also depart. Their holes are filled, and all the powder is poured into the victory vase or into another new vase adorned with cloth, which is placed on a litter. It is held by a yoginī wearing clothes given by the patron, and the vase on the litter is escorted forward by the master or a disciple with the sound of bells, auspicious songs, music, umbrellas, victory banners and so on.

Sitting on the bank of a great river or of the ocean, one makes a maṇḍala of white scents on ground smeared with cow dung. One visualizes Vajrasattva at the center of a lotus; on the petals of the directions, east and so on, going counterclockwise, and on the petals of the intermediate directions, Īśāna's and so on, going clockwise, are in order Ananta, Vāsuki, Takṣaka, Padma, Mahāpadma, Śaṅkhapāla, Karkoṭa and Anavatapta, white, blue, red, green, yellow, smoke-colored, green and yellow. From the navel up they have human form, with palms joined and hoods over their crowns, facing the Blessed One, each embraced by the left arm of a beautiful consort like himself. With mantras that insert the name of each in turn, such as oṃ āḥ vajra baruṇāya hūṃ svāhā and oṃ āḥ ānantāya hūṃ svāhā, one properly worships them with white flowers. Showing the left hand in the gesture of a hood and stretching out the right hand, one recites the nāga torma mantra: oṃ ānanta vāsuki takṣaka karkoṭa padma mahāpadma śaṃkhapāla kulika pāladevati mahādevati somaśikhi mahāsomaśikhi daṇḍadhara mahādaṇḍadhara apālāya hūṃ luṇaḍananda ubananta sāgara mahāsāgara tapta mahātapta śrīkānti mahākānti ratnakānti surupa mahāsurūpa bhadrāhika mahodhara śili mahāśili oṃ bakṣa āgaccha āgaccha mahānāga adhipati sarvaḥ bhurbhuva phaiṃ hūṃ svāhā. Giving them milk and a milk torma, one worships them. One should present the colored powder to them, imagining that they receive it with great reverence, and offer it into the water. The vase that carried the colored powder is also filled with water and the nāgas are invited into it in just the same way; with its water one sprinkles the maṇḍala house and so on, for the sake of pacification. The maṇḍala house is smeared with cow dung and a feast is given to young girls. This is the concluding of the maṇḍala.

This concludes the explanation of the seventeenth chapter.

To answer the fourth question, which begins, "By what master, and how, is it to be done?", the tantra speaks the passage beginning "Then." In it, the words from "disciplined" up to "should be known" give the characteristics of the master. The words from "by the master" up to "virtuous" give the characteristics of the disciple. The remainder, beginning "with palms joined," gives the number of the empowerments together with their rites, including the fourth empowerment as well. "Pleasant" means pleasant in speech. After "toward sentient beings as toward his sons," the words to be supplied are "he engages." "His mind endowed with the seal of equality" means that this mind is sealed by equality: it is a mind of love toward enemies, friends and servants alike. "Faculties" and so on means a body with all its faculties complete. "Knows the reality of the meaning of empowerment" means that he knows the empowerment and also the reality of its meaning. "Constantly and continually attends to the places" means one who attends to the sacred places and so on. By these words is meant one who meditates on the deity through the two stages. The characteristics of the disciple, given in the passage beginning "by the master," are easy to understand. "These" and so on: "accomplished" and so on means perfectly accomplished, because it is taught in the single way of the tathāgatas of the past, the future and the present. The activities to be done upon receiving empowerment are given in the passage beginning "Just as." The results of empowerment and the rest are given in the passage beginning "in a vessel."

Here the summarized meaning is this. First one uses a colored-powder maṇḍala as described above, though a maṇḍala on a painted scroll or the like will also do, and makes the offering of the five offerings. One examines well the disciples, who stay outside the site of the maṇḍala and have been placed in the yoga of the deity. They offer a maṇḍala, hold flower garlands, and kneel with their knees on the ground. The master draws them into his own body through his mouth, sends them out through the vajra path, and places them in the lotus of the goddess. With the light rays of the seed in his heart he invites the tathāgatas, who enter through his mouth together with their wisdom consorts and melt, and so confer empowerment on the disciples. Then he brings them out from there in the form of glorious Cakrasaṃvara and places them near the east door of the maṇḍala. They make supplication by reciting:

From the ocean of existence, terrifying
with birth, old age, death and sickness, with makaras and the rest,
you alone deliver us:
my Teacher, you of great joy,
who show the way of great awakening,
Great Protector, I seek you.
Grant me that very pledge;
grant me, too, the awakening mind;
the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha,
grant me these three refuges.
Into the supreme city of great liberation,
Protector, please lead me.

To them the master says:

Come here, gentle one! The rite
of the Mahāyāna way of mantra conduct
I shall rightly explain to you.
You are a vessel of the great way.
The buddhas who arise in the three times,
possessors of vajra body, speech and mind,
through the powers of the vajra mantra
rightly attained wisdom without equal.
Through the unequaled practice of mantra,
Śākyasiṃha and the other supreme ones
conquered the host of Māra,
greatly powerful and greatly fierce;
they understood the world that follows after,
turned the wheel, and passed into nirvāṇa.
Therefore, gentle one, set your mind on this
in order to attain omniscience.

Having said this, he has them rightly take it up, making them recite three times:

I go for refuge to the Three Jewels;
I confess each and every wrong;
I rejoice in the virtue of beings;
I hold buddhahood and awakening in mind.

To those who have taken the common vows, if they seek the vows from the master, and who supplicate him again by reciting,

Having granted me, Protector, the sublime
empowerment of the irreversible wheel,
please teach me the reality of the wheel's deities,
the activities of the master,
the pledges of all the buddhas,
and the supremely secret vows.
To accomplish the aims of all sentient beings
I shall always become a master,

he has them recite three times: "All buddhas and bodhisattvas, think of me! I, who am named such and such, from this time onward until I reach the heart of awakening:

Just as the protectors of the three times
made certain of perfect awakening,
so shall I generate the sublime,
unsurpassed awakening mind.
The three trainings, the discipline of vows,
the gathering of virtuous qualities,
and the discipline of working for beings,
I shall each hold firmly.
The Buddha, the Dharma and the supreme Saṅgha,
the unsurpassed Three Jewels:
the vow that arises from buddha yoga,
from this day on I shall hold.
Vajra, bell and mudrā,
the reality of each I shall hold.
Arisen from the great vajra family,
the master too I shall hold.
In the pleasing pledges
of the great jewel family,
six times every day
I shall always give the four gifts.
In the pure, great lotus family,
arisen from great awakening,
the outer and the secret and the three vehicles,
the true Dharma, I shall each hold.
Arisen from the great action family,
the act of offering as far as I am able,
fully endowed with all the vows,
