all these I shall each hold.
To accomplish the aims of all sentient beings,
having generated the sublime,
unsurpassed awakening mind,
and having taken all the vows,
I shall ferry across those who have not crossed,
I shall free those who are not free,
I shall give relief to those without relief,
and I shall set sentient beings in nirvāṇa."

Having thus had them first generate the mind set on awakening, the master protects those who have taken the vows with the all-purpose mantra. He visualizes at their hearts, throats and crowns the letters hūṃ, āḥ and oṃ, black, red and white, resting on sun and moon above a vajra, a lotus and a wheel. Then, reciting them, he touches their hearts, throats and crowns with the vajra held in his right hand, moistened with scented water. With the mantras of flowers and the rest he offers flowers at the crown, incense and a lamp in front, and fine scents at the heart. Then he gives the disciples, who face north, tooth sticks of aśvattha or another tree: straight, twelve finger-widths long, free of insects, unsplit and unblemished, washed in fragrant water, with a flower garland tied at the tip.

The disciples, facing east or north on a square maṇḍala four cubits across smeared with cow dung and the like, say oṃ vajra hasaha and chew the tip of the tooth stick; then, with the unsplit end turned upward, they should toss it forward without concern. It should be explained to them that, according to whether it falls within the maṇḍala pointing east, north, west, south, up or down, the accomplishment of pacifying, of enriching, of magnetizing, of fierce rites, of the knowledge holders, or of the underworld will come about. If it falls outside, there is no accomplishment.

Then the master gives them water, arisen from vaṃ and recited over with that same syllable, to rinse their mouths, and has them drink three palmfuls of scented water over which the all-purpose mantra has been recited seven times, while saying oṃ hrīḥ suviśuddha sarvadharmāḥ pāpanicasya viśodhaya sarvavikalpāna apanaya hūṃ phaṭ.

Then, to those sitting outside, he gives many blades of kuśa grass with unbroken tips, arisen from hūṃ and recited over seven times with it, to serve as a seat, and likewise a single one arisen from the syllable dhīḥ to serve as a pillow, each recited over with its own syllable and with the all-purpose mantra; he gives them with the mantra oṃ vajra tikṣṇā dhaṃ. Then he takes a red thread the length of the disciple's body, folded in three, arisen from hūṃ and recited over seven times with it and with the all-purpose mantra, ties three knots in it, and binds it on their left upper arms while saying oṃ buddha maitri rakṣa rakṣa sarvāna svāhā. Having protected them well, he arouses great enthusiasm in them and has them listen to this:

Whatever wrongs were done before,
through many tens of millions of eons,
all of them are exhausted
on seeing a maṇḍala such as this.
Those whose understanding
is utterly stainless in this supreme conduct
are delivered from the lower realms,
the source of every suffering.
Great beings, today you have found
a gain beyond compare,
through which you dwell here in the teaching
of all the victors and their heirs,
fully embraced by them all:
you will be born as great beings.
By this you will take birth
within the Great Vehicle.
The Great Vehicle, great source,
this supreme and glorious path:
if you travel by it,
you will become tathāgatas.

After that he protects them with the Kuṇḍalin mantra and explains the Kuṇḍalin mantra so that they may recite it. Saying, "Tell me in the morning," he has them lie down in the lion's posture inside the palace at the edge of the maṇḍala ground, screened off by a curtain, or else somewhere else. Then he himself enters the maṇḍala house and should recite the Kuṇḍalin mantra for protection. This is the rite of the disciples' preparatory stay.

After that, in the morning, the guru offers the torma, comes out of the maṇḍala house, and asks the disciples about their dreams. If there are dreams that indicate nonvirtue, such as falling from a mountain, he protects them with the all-purpose mantra. At the time of entering the maṇḍala house, the disciples arouse enthusiasm with oṃ praviśa bhagavān mahāsukha mokṣapuraṃ sarvasiddhi sukhapradaṃ paramasukha uttama siddhya jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ prasiddhya svā, and are led in. Going clockwise, in the form of the Lord of the Wheel, they stand at the west and the other doors; facing the east and the other doors of the maṇḍala, they say oṃ vajra utghaṭya samaya praveśaya hūṃ and open the doors. They throw a flower garland into the maṇḍala with the mantra oṃ paramasukhāśaya sulālita vilāsa namita namitair bhagavānataṃ jaḥ hūṃ vam hoḥ hi hi hi hi hrīḥ pratīcchaḥ kusumāñjali nātha hoḥ, take it back, and bind it on their own crowns with the thought of the garland empowerment. Intensely conceiving the Lord of the Wheel, now manifest before them, as the guru, they receive from him all the empowerments that will be explained, beginning with the garland empowerment which he grants; and they also obtain, according to the rite, authorization for all the activities of a master. Then, in order that they in turn may admit disciples, they request the accomplishments of the maṇḍala, preceded by a praise of that very reality which is great bliss, the nature of method and wisdom, and they also perform the blessing of truth, saying:

Arisen from space, its very sign,
supreme, without beginning or end,
of the nature of the great vajra being:
today accomplish me as Akṣobhyavajra.
Great accomplishment, supreme over all,
great lord (Maheśvara), god above the gods,
king of all who hold the vajra:
accomplish for me the changeless supreme.
Faultless, beyond permanence,
passion answering every passion,
great passion and great joy:
may that very Blessed One accomplish me.
Utterly pure, supreme of all dharmas,
tathāgata liberated from the beginning,
Samantabhadra, self of all,
bodhisattva, accomplish me.
Great accomplishment, supreme over all,
great lord, supreme seal,
as the Vajragarbha of the excellent great vajra
accomplish me.
Pervader of the minds of all beings,
dwelling in the hearts of all beings,
the very father of all beings,
supreme desire of the supreme pledge:
by the truth through which there is wisdom,
the maṇḍala, self of method and wisdom,
by that truth, O Protector,
fulfill my wishes completely.
Phenomena are like reflections,
clear and pure, without turbidity,
ungraspable and inexpressible,
truly arisen from causes and actions;
that very suchness surely emerges.
By this truth, may the maṇḍala
be as clear as a reflection,
and may all the disciples see it unobscured.

This should be recited. This is the rite of the master's entry.

Then the disciple should be bathed and made clean with fragrances and so forth. Outside the curtain, clothe the disciple in red garments over which the all-purpose mantra has been recited, and visualize the disciple in the form of the Lord of the Wheel (Cakreśvara). With the mantras oṃ sarba tathāgata anuttara bodhyālaṃkara vastra pūja megha samudra spharaṇa samaye hūṃ, oṃ vajra rakṣa hūṃ and oṃ vajra uṣṇīṣa hūṃ phaṭ, put on the lower garment, the upper garment and the crown-knot. With a red or yellow cloth over which the all-purpose mantra has been recited, tie on the blindfold, saying oṃ cakṣu bandha vara mana ya hūṃ. Saying ā khaṃ vīra hūṃ, have the disciple hold a garland of flowers, and sprinkle the disciple who has offered the fee with the all-purpose water.

At the heart, the throat and the crown, in the center of a vajra, a lotus and a wheel, upon sun and moon, visualize the syllables hūṃ, āḥ and oṃ, black, red and white, and with their rays of light make the body thoroughly radiant. Saying hūṃ, place the disciples at the eastern door of the maṇḍala, and, so as to admit those held by the karmavajra into the maṇḍala, put the question: "O you who delight in joy, who are you?" The disciple should answer: "I am the fortunate one, great bliss."

Then the disciple says oṃ sarva yoga citta utpādayāmi and generates the awakening mind. Then, holding the vajra at the disciple's heart together with the flowers, the master says oṃ surate samaya stvaṃ hoḥ siddhya vajra yathā sukhaṃ, and says: "Today you will be blessed by all the Tathāgatas. You must not speak of this to those who have not entered the supreme maṇḍala of the great secret of all the Tathāgatas, nor may you lose faith." Having said this, with oṃ ā khaṃ vīra hūṃ lead the disciple inside the curtain.

Then, holding the vajra at the crown of the head and ringing the bell, say:

If you should ever speak
of this pledge-vajra of yours
to anyone at all, your head will shatter.

Then, holding the vajra at the heart, give this command:

oṃ Today Vajrasattva himself
has truly entered your heart.
Should you speak of this way,
in that very instant he will tear it apart and depart.

Then, over the water in the lotus vessel standing upon the victory vase, mixed with the five nectars, recite oṃ pañca amṛta udaka ṭhaḥ seven times, and say:

This is your water of hell:
if the pledge is transgressed, it burns;
if the pledge is kept, it grants accomplishment.
Drink the water of vajra nectar!

Saying this, hold the vessel with the ring finger and thumb of the left hand, put its mouth to the disciple's mouth, and have the disciple drink. Then say:

You must not despise me;
you must not let the pledges decline,
or you will go to hell and the like.

Having said this, mix together a cloth stained with dust mixed with the five nectars and the five lamps, dried cat dung, and guggulu, and pour them onto a fire of charnel-ground charcoal in a lotus vessel. Holding it in the right hand and ringing the bell with the left, stand in the posture with the right leg extended, and visualize yourself as having the nature of glorious Cakrasaṃvara. Recite tiṣṭha mahā krodha aveśaya hūṃ śrī herukāya vidmahe vihyārājāya dhimahe svāhā until the disciple is filled, and fumigate with incense the disciple, who has been made into the deity and joins the palms at the heart, and cause the deity to descend.

If the one who has descended leaps about wildly, then visualize above the disciple, in order, each one above the other, a Mount Meru of vajra nature, the maṇḍala of Mahendra, a yellow five-pronged vajra, the syllable jaḥ, and Vairocana, and so turn it back.

When the descent has taken place in this way, with the rays of light from the seed syllable of the disciple's vajra mind, arouse the seed syllable of vajra speech, visualize on the tongue a red syllable āḥ resting on a moon, wreathed in a blazing garland, and say: "Speak, vajra!" The disciple will then declare the virtue and non-virtue of himself and of others.

After that, invite the deity that has descended with the rays of light from your own heart, and meditate that it dissolves into the seed syllable at your heart. Holding the vajra at the crown of the disciple's head, dismiss it with hūṃ haḥ āḥ jhaiṃ muḥ, preceded by the verse beginning "oṃ You have accomplished."

Then ask the disciple, who faces the wheel, "How does it appear to your eyes?" According to the white, yellow, red or black appearances the disciple describes, the pacifying, enriching, subjugating or fierce rites will respectively be accomplished, and this should be explained to the disciples.

Then, joining the palms toward the Blessed One, say:

Into this true maṇḍala
I shall admit the disciple.
May the deities and the order of the families
accord with the disciple's merit.
How will accomplishment come to this one?
Of which family is this one a fit vessel?
Whatever this one's merit and power may be,
so may it be revealed in the maṇḍala.

Having thus made the blessing of truth, take from the flower garland held earlier one stainless flower, empower it with the syllable traṃ, and with the syllable hūṃ cast it into the maṇḍala, saying oṃ heruka vajra puṣpa pratīccha hūṃ. The deity upon whom the flower falls is the disciple's family, and this should be pointed out to the disciple.

Then pick up that flower, tie it on the crown of the disciple's head, and say:

Great mighty hero,
take hold of it fully here.

Then visualize at the disciple's heart the lord upon a moon, on the forehead raṃ, and on the two eyes two blazing oṃ syllables, and say:

oṃ Today Vajrasattva himself
strives to open your eyes.
Since it opens every eye,
it is the unsurpassed vajra eye.

Saying this, remove the blindfold and say, "O vajra, look!" Then explain the positions together with the names of the deities. This is the admission of the disciple.

After that, the disciple who has offered the fee, having first prostrated, sets the knees on the ground, joins the palms, and makes this request:

Just as Bodhivajra
bestowed the great offering upon the buddhas,
so for my protection too,
grant me today the space-vajra.

When the disciple has made this request, draw the disciple in through your mouth, send the disciple out by the vajra path, and place the disciple in the lotus of the goddess. Then, instantly and without interval after emptiness, the disciple is the body of Cakrasaṃvara arisen from hūṃ, two-armed, together with his wisdom consort, of the nature of Akṣobhya. Imagine that the Tathāgatas and the others, together with their wisdom consorts, invited by the rays of light from the seed syllable at your own heart, melt in great passion, enter through the door of Vairocana, come forth by the vajra path, and confer empowerment upon the disciple with what is of the nature of conventional seminal fluid. Having brought the disciple out of the lotus, meditate that the disciple is empowered and worshipped by those very deities, and by the offerings and honors of many kinds of embodied forms, hands, feet and the like, that issue from them.

Then, with the water held in the lotus vessel, poured from the vases of Vijaya and the others, regard it as Akṣobhya, born from a vajra that arises from hūṃ and is blessed by the syllable hūṃ, become one with the wisdom being invited by the rays of light from your own seed syllable, and worship it properly. From its transformation, with the tip of a leafy twig held together with a vajra in the right hand, imagining that the yoginīs are singing songs, and with auspicious songs going before, say:

The great vajra empowerment,
to which the three realms pay homage,
arisen from the abode of the three secrets
of all the buddhas, I shall bestow.

oṃ cakra vīra tvaṃ abhiṣiñcami

Saying this, confer the empowerment. This is the empowerment of water.

Then have the disciple make this request:

The crown, sovereignty over the three worlds,
which accomplishes the aims of all sentient beings,
abode of the qualities of Vajrasattva:
O you who possess great compassion, grant it to me.

Then visualize that the disciple, from the transformation of a jewel arisen from aṃ and blessed by it, is the body of Ratnasambhava become one with the wisdom being, and likewise that the crown has transformed into Ratnasambhava. Reciting the mantras of the Blessed Lady beginning oṃ sarba buddha, and the heart mantra of the Blessed One and the others, place it upon the disciple's head. This is the crown.

Then, when the disciple makes this request:

That by which my ignorance
is dispelled and wisdom is attained,
proclaimed as the nature of the five wisdoms:
grant me the vajra discipline,

visualize that the disciple, from the transformation of a lotus arisen from jṛṃ and blessed by it, is the body of Amitābha inseparable from the wisdom being, and likewise that the vajra too has transformed into the body of Amitābha. Place it in the disciple's right hand and say:

Today glorious Heruka
and the heroines empower you.
This is the vajra of all the buddhas:
hold it, for the sake of perfect accomplishment.

This is the vajra.

Then, when the disciple makes this request:

That by whose attainment everywhere
I become endowed with wonder,
whose nature is the perfection of wisdom:
grant me the empowerment of the bell,

visualize that the disciple, from the transformation of a sword arisen from khaṃ and blessed by it, is the body of Amoghasiddhi inseparable from the wisdom being, and likewise that the bell too has transformed into Amoghasiddhi. Hand it to the disciple's left hand, saying:

This is declared to follow
the bell-voice of all the yoginīs.
The Victors hold it to be supreme awakening:
you too must hold it always.

Having handed it over, have the disciple make the gesture of embracing at the navel with the hands holding the vajra and the vajra bell. This is the bell.

Then, when the disciple makes this request:

Upon me, a vessel of the inconceivable Dharma,
confer the sacred empowerment,

visualize that the disciple, from the transformation of a wheel arisen from bhrūṃ and blessed by it, is the body of Vairocana inseparable from the wisdom being. Holding your own vajra and bell with both your hands at the crown of the disciple's head, say oṃ śrī cakra adhipati śrī heruka vīra tvaṃ abhiṣiñcami, "You are Such-and-such Vajra." This is the name.

Here the names of Vajrasattva are: Supreme First, Incomparable, Joy, Equal Taste, Victorious, Alala, Being, Great Bliss, Limbless, Wisdom, and Innate Vajra. The word "vajra" should be joined to each of them. It is the same with the names of the six Tathāgatas.

Those of Akṣobhya are: Laughter, Grace, Free of Joy, Play, Majestic Bearing, Self-Arisen, Aversion, he, Compassion, Heart, the Maker of hūṃ, and Akṣobhyavajra.

Those of Vairocana are: the Maker of oṃ, Permanent, Tathāgata, Illusion, Buddha, Form, and Body Vajra.

Those of Ratnasambhava are: Jewel, Wish-Fulfilling Gem, Sky-Goer, Space, Splendor, Sun, Light Ray, and Pride Vajra.

Those of Amitābha are: Desire, Avalokiteśvara, Dharma, Padma, Vajra Sprout, Lotus Sprout, Compassion Sprout, Supreme Joy, Joy of Utmost Joy, Ananta, and Speech Vajra.

Those of Amoghasiddhi are: Unfailing, Paramāśva, Pledge, Perfection, Action, Vāyu, Touch, and Jealousy Vajra.

For women, those of Vajrasattvī are: Vajra Impetus, Vajra Graceful Lady, Vajra Intoxicated Lady, Vajra Koṅkaṇā, Vajra Drawn-Face Lady, Vajra Banner Lady, Vajra Garland Lady, and Vajra Arising Lady.

Those of Akṣobhya are: Vajranairātmyā, Vajra Hand Lady, Vajra Garland Lady, Vajra Coming Lady, Vajra Māmakī, and Vajra Joy Lady.

Those of Vairocana are: Ḍākinī, Vajra White Lady, Vajra Graceful Lady, Vajra Locanā, and Delusion Joy Lady.

Those of Ratnasambhava are: Vajra Necklace Lady, Vajra Consoling Lady, Vajra Blazing Lady, Vajra Garland Lady, Vajra Sky Lady, and Sky Joy Lady.

Those of Amitābha are: Vajra Desire Lady, Vajra Song Lady, Vajra Bliss Wonder Lady, Vajra Vetālī, Vajra Intelligence Lady, Vajra Laughing Lady, Vajra Pāṇḍaravāsinī, and Desire Joy Lady.

Those of Amoghasiddhi are: Vajra Proud Lady, Vajra Illusion Lady, Vajra Wild Lady, Vajra Tārā, Vajra Lady of the Dance, Vajra Pledge Lady, and Vajra Joy Lady.

For the crown and the rest that are spoken of in every case, and also for the empowerments that are yet to be explained, one should regard the Tathāgatas and the others dwelling in the sky, holding in their hands vases filled with the nectar of wisdom, as conferring empowerment upon the disciple. With auspicious songs going before, reciting "The great vajra empowerment" and the rest, one should confer the empowerment in the same way as the water empowerment.

If the disciple does not seek the master's vow, then, preceded by the common vow, one should give these very stages as the cause for attaining the five wisdoms through the dispelling of ignorance. Through that, such disciples become fit for hearing, explaining and so on the kriyā and caryā tantras themselves. This is stated in the Vajramālā:

By that which brings one to obtain
the empowerment of the five pure buddhas,
through the stages of dispelling ignorance,
one comes to attain the five wisdoms.

For one who does seek it, preceded by both vows, one should also give those empowerments that are yet to be explained.

Then, for the disciple who makes this request:

The mantra empowerment, supreme wisdom,
which bestows accomplishment, grant it to me,

recite the heart mantra of the Blessed One yourself one hundred and eight times, and say:

Having filled the moon-like face,
the mantra body, thoroughly purified,
bestowing accomplishment, for the disciple's sake
is given through the lineage of the guru's mouth.

Having given this, the mantra should be uttered, and you say:

I have bestowed it, Blessed One:
please draw near to this one.

The disciple too, receiving it with these words:

I have received it, Blessed One:
please draw near to me,

should recite it three times. This is the mantra empowerment.

Then, for the disciple who makes the request with the verse "the nature of the five wisdoms," meditated upon in the form of Vajrasattva, say:

This is all the buddhas,
abiding in the hand of glorious Heruka.
Firm in the discipline of glorious Heruka,
you too must hold it always.

Saying this, hand over the vajra. The disciple too should take it, saying oṃ sarba tathāgata siddhya vajra samaya tiṣṭha eṣa śvaṃ dharayāmi oṃ hīḥ hi hi hi hi hūṃ. This is the vajra discipline.

Then, for the disciple who makes this request:

For the purification of the conduct
of beings of varied dispositions,
the means of accomplishing the aims of all sentient beings:
grant me the discipline of conduct,

having made the disciple into the body of the Blessed One, give the khaṭvāṅga, the ḍamaru and the lotus vessel, over which the heart mantra of the Blessed One has been recited three times, saying:

Khaṭvāṅga, little drum and vessel,
truly abiding outside and within:
this seal of glorious Heruka
I give; may the hero take hold of it.

This is the discipline of conduct.

Then, visualizing in the body of the Blessed One the disciple who makes this request:

Today I have entered the buddha family:
O Protector, give me a prophecy,

adopt yourself the demeanor of a Tathāgata. Holding the corner of your outer robe at the heart with the left fist, and making the gesture of fearlessness with the right, say:

I, lord of the three wheels,
the very nature of all the buddhas,
prophesy you, Tathāgata,
as glorious Heruka.

You will be accomplished as the Tathāgata called glorious Heruka. samaya stvaṃ bhur bhuva svāṃ

Great lord of the kingship of the three realms,
source of qualities, endowed with glory,
into the supreme city, having subdued
all the hosts of Māra, you have entered.

Saying this, the prophecy should be given. This is the prophecy.

Then, for the disciple who makes this request:

That by which one comes to perform
the signless activity for beings in this way,
the great irreversible empowerment:
great hero, grant it to me,

seat the disciple on a throne or similar seat and visualize the disciple in the body of Vajrasattva. Confer the empowerment by the rite taught for the water empowerment, and meditate on Akṣobhya as the crown ornament. Dissolve the other Tathāgatas and the goddesses into the disciple in the manner of wisdom beings, bless the disciple with oṃ supratiṣṭha vajre svāhā, and worship with all the offerings, flowers and the rest. This is the irreversible empowerment.

Then the permission should be given:

For the yogin who knows suchness
there is nothing whatever not to be done.
Hero, having then known that suchness,
enjoy the five sense pleasures.
I am the nature of all heroes,
I am the very self of all goddesses;
therefore all my actions
are worship of myself, glorious Heruka.
Sentient beings, whose nature is a reflection,
are of the character of dependent arising.
Having understood the three realms in this way,
the peerless one should work for the aims of sentient beings.

This is the permission.

Then the disciple who has done what was to be done
offers the fee to the guru,
praises him, bows with the whole self,
and should gather the holy ones.

Then the disciple makes this request:

Teacher, refuge of those without refuge,
Cakrasaṃvara of the Wheel, protect me!
You whose great compassion is without cause,
protector of the protectorless, be gracious.
Just as you are the Great Being,
the all-pervading lord, so make me too.
Sunk in the mass of mire that is saṃsāra,
I am without refuge: protect me!

When the disciple has made this request, the guru takes the pledge consort whom the disciple has offered to the guru, one endowed with beauty and the rest, and in a place made secluded from people by curtains and the like, accomplishes her in the form of Vajravārāhī and himself in the form of glorious Cakrasaṃvara. With the preparatory consecration of lotus and vajra going before, he says oṃ sarba tathāgata anurāgaṇa vajra svabhāva ātmako'haṃ and enters into union. The Tathāgatas, Vairocana and the others, in union with Locanā and the others, invited by the rays of light from the seed syllable at his heart, he causes to enter into himself. Melted by great passion, they go together with his own awakening mind through the avadhūtī, and he makes them steady in the vajra jewel. Having experienced the innate, he withdraws the vajra jewel from the lotus with the thumb and ring finger and presses out that fluid of his own awakening mind, which has the nature of the fluid of the Tathāgatas, issuing from the vajra jewel. Into the mouth of the disciple, who has the nature of vajra speech, in such a way that, because of the blindfold and the like, it does not become an object of the disciple's sight, he should give it, saying:

This is the great and wondrous pledge
of the heroes and the goddesses:
taste the sacred bliss
in which the five elements are of one taste.
Just as the vajra holders of the past and others
empowered the sons of the buddhas,
so, pleased with the excellent awakening mind,
I shall empower you.

and reciting also the heart mantra of the Blessed One. The disciple, with the thought that all the Tathāgatas, Vairocana and the others, are gathered there, says aho mahā ta ati sukhaṃ, "It is the Teacher's command," and drinks it. The wisdom consort too rises and, quite naked, places into the disciple's mouth the drop of honey that rests in her own lotus, and the disciple likewise drinks it. This is the secret empowerment.

After that, for the disciple who makes this request:

The empowerment that gathers all accomplishments:
please bestow it upon me,

the guru places at the disciple's left side that consort, or another wisdom consort who abides in the pledges and vows and is endowed with beauty and youth, and hands her over, saying:

The goddess of the Great Seal,
she who bestows endless bliss:
in order to hold her, son, in every way
make unsurpassed offering.

When the disciple makes this request:

That very goddess, supreme wisdom,
grant to me for perfect accomplishment,

the guru should say to the disciple:

In eating the seed of awakening,
hero, are you eager or not?
Truly born from the five families,
worship the consort with reverence.
Urine, flesh, blood and the rest,
now fully partake of them.
And further, as you wish,
son, speak the excellent pledge.

The disciple should reply:

In eating the seed of awakening,
goddess, why would I not be eager?
Why would I not always practice with reverence?
Why would I not worship the woman's lotus?

She too, showing her unclothed lotus anointed with saffron and other fine fragrances, should say:

How wonderful! This lotus of mine
is truly endowed with every bliss.
Whoever relies upon it according to the rite,
before that one I abide.
Pleasing the perfect buddhas and the rest,
act in the lotus as is prescribed.
You yourself are the king of great bliss,
abiding always right here.

and, saying bhañja mokṣa hoḥces, she should show it.

Then the practitioner, generating himself in the body of Cakrasaṃvara and her in the form of Vajravārāhī, with the preparatory consecration of vajra and lotus going before, stimulates the channel in the heart of the lotus with the finger and the tip of the tongue, blessed by the syllable hrīḥ. When trembling and the like arise, together with that he recites the mantra oṃ śī śī śī ho ho ho svāhā and says oṃ sarba tathāgata anurāgaṇa vajra svabhāba ātmako'haṃ; his body being blessed by the Tathāgatas invited by the rays of light from the seed syllable at his heart, he should bring about joy.

In this way, retaining the awakening mind as far as he is able according to the guru's instructions, he attends closely to the divisions of joy and the rest. What has fallen he should take from the lotus by means of the rasanā itself and eat.

Then the Blessed Lady, delighted,
reciting the bhañja mokṣa mantra,
places her left foot upon his crown
and should show her permission:
Son, from this day onward
until the heart of awakening,
the aims of all sentient beings, which are like a dream,
accomplish with your whole self.

This is the wisdom-knowledge empowerment.

After that, the experience gained from the wisdom consort should be explained, so that he may meditate on that very experience as endowed with the seven limbs. This is the fourth empowerment.

After that, the guru places the hand of that wisdom consort in the disciple's hand, holds both of them with his own left hand, and, setting his right hand holding the vajra on the crown of the disciple's head, says:

With you as witnesses here,
I entrust this one to this one.

Making the Tathāgatas the witnesses, he says:

If you abandon this consort,
you will not become a buddha by any other means.
This is the unsurpassed
consort discipline of all the buddhas,
the supreme of the supreme among disciplines.
With delight and gentleness practice the consort discipline.
Whatever deluded one transgresses it
has no supreme accomplishment.

Saying this, he should bestow the consort discipline. This is the consort discipline.

When, through the four empowerments that have been taught,
the one who keeps the discipline is empowered,
for the sake of obtaining the inconceivable Dharma,
then, for that very one, that is here.

This is the explanation of the eighteenth chapter.

To answer the question, "What is the certainty of time? And likewise, how is death to be cheated?" the text says "Then" and so forth. What was taught earlier are chiefly the signs of death at its proper time, based on the wind alone. These are mostly the signs of untimely death, based on one's own body and on what is outside it. That is the difference, and it is easy to understand. "By those endowed with the instructions as they are" means by the stages that do not depart from what has been taught: through gems, mantras and medicines thoroughly applied.

Likewise, "by suchness" means by meditation. "By the Dharma" means by practicing generosity and the rest.

When one is unable to avert death, then the yogin should transfer and abandon the body. This is taught by "Moreover" and so forth. "By the application of expelling and filling" means by the application of releasing and filling. "Various" means when the outer and inner signs of death are observed. "Breath" means the winds. "Cut off and cut off" means that they abandon their own places. That is taught by "the time of death" and so forth. "Having expelled, it transfers" means that, having come forth from the body, it transfers: the consciousness departs. "Nine doors" are the two eyes, the two ears, the two nostrils, the mouth, the sexual organ and the secret place; these are what are known as the nine doors. "Through complete pacification it becomes pacified" means that when, through habituation to the vase breath, the wind has become stable, stability will arise. "The stealing of consciousness" means that the consciousness departs. "By going beyond elsewhere" is intended as the manner and mode of yogins who have gained mastery. Those who have attained stability in the generation and completion stages have actualized the Great Seal (Mahāmudrā) with this present body, and so no fear of any kind is possible for them. For that very reason this method is for the other kind.

"Āli and kāli" are the winds of the left and right nostrils. "The letter with a half" means hi and the letter kā with its vowel removed. The seed of wind is the syllable yaṃ. "The mount of consciousness is wind" means the wind of consciousness. "The door of wind" means it is the door of wind. "By thinking": "visualize" is the word to be supplied.

Here the instruction is this. First, settle in meditative equipoise, with no other activity for the time being. At the aperture of Brahmā visualize kṣuṃ; below it hūṃ; between the eyebrows hūṃ; on the anther of the lotus of great bliss haṃ; at the opening of the throat suṃ; at the sexual organ smchuṃ; at the anus kṣuṃ; and at the openings of the ears and nostrils yuṃ. Having visualized these seed syllables, at the root of the navel is the form of yaṃ. By the hook of the light rays of the inner fire (caṇḍālī), and also by filling and the rest, the winds are thoroughly drawn in from the twenty-four places, the crown of the head and the others, and the winds dwelling in the ten places become gathered into a ball at the secret wheel. In the same way, join the blazing hook of the kindled inner fire to the wind maṇḍala at the heart, and, according to the guru's instruction, recite the mantra hūṃ, set between two maṇḍalas arisen from a pair of yuṃ syllables joined face to face, dwelling above and below the mind.

Then, having become certain of death through definite outer and inner signs of death, one should perform the transference. If, while life still remains, the consciousness departs, one will be born in hell and the other lower realms.

"With which" refers to the consciousness, which fully bestows the accomplishment of liberation. "The navel" means that if it goes by the navel, one becomes a god who enjoys desire. "By the drop" means the aperture between the eyebrows; "an embodied form" means one who enjoys form. "Of the upper" means of the aperture of Brahmā; "into the formless realm" means one who enjoys formlessness. "That is the division of going to the good": "the good" is an indication, and the divisions of going to places that are not good destinies should also be known. Having taught this in brief, the text teaches it at length with "yakṣa" and so forth. "Of the liberated" means by those who go by transference, because they transfer as they wish. "When death has arrived at its time" means that one should transfer only when the time of death has actually arrived.

This is the explanation of the nineteenth chapter.

To answer the two questions beginning "How many are the limbs of the four ages?" the text says "Then" and so forth. "Eon" means an intermediate eon. "Twenty-eight thousand" means eight thousand in addition to twenty thousand. Therefore seventeen hundred thousand plus twenty thousand is the measure of the Age of Perfection (Kṛtayuga). In the same way, the others are reckoned by tenths, together with the measure of the junctions at their boundaries. "Protector" is an address to Vajrapāṇi in his very role as chief of the hosts of yakṣas.

This is the explanation of the twentieth chapter.

To answer the question, "How should the conduct be practiced?" the text says "Then" and so forth. "Gone beyond" is said because it is the very cause of going to the far shore of the ocean of saṃsāra, the result being figuratively applied to the cause. Alternatively, it means "of those who go beyond": they have gone beyond. "By which" means by the conduct. "At the culmination of accomplishment" means the ultimate accomplishment, the accomplishment of the Great Seal; "practitioner" means by the practitioner. "Common" means to the kriyā tantras and the rest. "The holy guru" means from the holy guru; "having served" means from having served.

Here the summarized meaning is this. Having pleased the guru according to the rite, the yogin who has gained outstanding familiarity with the generation and completion stages together with their oral instructions, who is able to take others into his care and to subdue them, and who gives away his wealth and the rest, up to his very body, without attachment, should supplicate the guru and the Blessed One, glorious Cakrasaṃvara and the others, and, with their permission, undertake any one of the three kinds of conduct.

Of these, the text speaks with regard to practicing the unelaborated conduct with "Before" and so forth, which is easy to understand. "The self of wisdom and method" means together with the wisdom consort who has attained transformation and the rest. Alternatively, "unequal" means the conduct of a madman. "Not together" means doing it here without moving among crowds, without seal and fixed abode, and without mental engagement. That very point will be explained with "like a quivering leaf." "Alone" means free of an outer consort. "With one-pointed mind" means having no thought other than the conduct undertaken. "A workshop, a hidden place": this discipline, practiced in that way, is superior to the conduct of a madman, because one does not speak even for the sake of food and alms. That is taught by "One should dwell in the yoga of the sage." Then, if one asks how the body should be sustained, the text says "food" and so forth. "Living by begging" means one should live on alms.

This is the explanation of the twenty-first chapter.

To answer the fourth question, beginning "How many are the yogin tantras?" the text says "Then" and so forth.

"Of the Great Vehicle" means the activity of the Great Vehicle. "Other" means of the śrāvaka vehicle. "All these were taught" means those that were taught concerning all of these. "The Lord of Sages" means by the lords of sages. "Having the nature of the three bodies" means by those who have the nature of the three bodies. "The teaching of the Sugata" is a summation: the meaning is that what is of this kind is the teaching of the Sugata.

If one asks with reference to whom it was taught, the text says "of the Vinaya" and so forth. "Teaching itself among the śrāvakas" means to persons of the śrāvaka lineage; the case ending is the seventh, the locative. "Teaching itself" means that it was taught for the sake of taming oneself, pacifying oneself, and so on. "Extensive exposition" means the sūtra collections that serve to explain other sūtra collections. "Of the bodhisattvas" means for the sake of those of the bodhisattva lineage. In "of the yoga and yoginī tantras," the sixth case, the genitive, stands in the sense of the first, the nominative. In "the domain of the buddhas," the words "with reference to sentient beings" are to be supplied; it means that this has become the domain of the buddhas alone. It is said that by these the way of mantra is taught for the sake of great wisdom. As it is said:

Mind itself is taught to be utterly pure;
great in its nature as the Great Vehicle,
it is the domain of the great beings.

And elsewhere too:

Though the aim is the same, through freedom from confusion,
through many means, and through absence of hardship,
and because it is directed to those of great wisdom,
the vehicle of mantra is especially exalted.

If one asks to whom this wondrous teaching is given, the text says "of lesser merit." The intent is that even beings of lesser merit gradually come close to being tamed. Or else: for the sake of beings of lesser merit, the mantra vehicle presents itself merely through the forms of deities. Relying on the form of the deity, one meditates on the Dharma body and the rest, and relying on that, one meditates on the union of wisdom and means, whose nature is emptiness and compassion. In their way of mantra, these clearly teach the path in two stages, the stage of generation and the stage of completion, whose nature is cause and result. Further, "one swiftly attains buddhahood" because, if one has diligence, one attains buddhahood in this very life.

To answer the question about consecration, the text says "Then" and so forth. "Of consecration" means for consecration. "Having gone to the far shore of the rite" means the rite fully completed. "The ten acts" means the empowerments of water and the rest; the meaning is that just as is done in empowering a disciple, so it should also be done for the image. "When people are satisfied, there is increase" means that through the satisfaction of the people the image will flourish, and by implication the patron as well. If one asks what comes from the flourishing of the image, the text says "through increase" and so forth. "From that which is auspicious" means that from auspiciousness having been brought about at the time of consecration, there comes its pacification. If one asks what comes from that, the text says "it is pacifying"; "pacifying" means protection for as long as it lasts, and restoration. "Because of a yoga of reviling" means from a blameworthy application, that is, from the performance of what is blameworthy and the like. "Harms and various sufferings arise" means harms and various sufferings: the suffering of an image is being cut, being torn down, and so on, and harm comes from fire, water, and so on.

The rite for this is as follows. In a maṇḍala of colored powders as taught, or in a painted maṇḍala, after the image or other object has been dissolved into emptiness, generate it just as it is. Visualize the form of the pledge being at its heart, together with its seed syllable. Then have the patron, adorned with new red garments and the like and holding flowers, take hold of the end of its flower garland, and have it anointed as one who has beheld the maṇḍala. Not far to the east of the maṇḍala, or in the northeast, the quarter of Īśāna, make a square platform two cubits high, of clay, or of the ground as it is, or plastered and so on. In its center draw a multicolored lotus with a door to the west, four cubits across and surrounded by a single line. Adorn it with a canopy and the like, set cymbals, songs and the rest before it, and decorate it. In its center place the image or other object, upon a wooden seat in the middle of which an eight-petaled lotus has been drawn, resting on a visualized moon disc. Preceded by water for welcoming and the other offerings, recite the all-purpose action mantra and also the syllable hūṃ seven times. Holding white mustard seed in the right and the left fists, circle it counterclockwise twice with each, saying oṃ sarba pāpaṃ dahana vajrāya sarbba satvasya sarbba pāpaṃ dahana svāhā, and so purify it; then cast the white mustard seed into the fire. Do the same circling clockwise. In the same way, circle water around it to the left and to the right, and likewise circle around it four bowls smeared all over with white paste, balls of cow dung together with shoots of dūrvā grass, balls of barley dough, and fish scales, and set them on the ground, not in the fire. Then touch the image or other object with the hands, and with the mantra of the Lord of the Wheel (Cakreśvara) anoint it with scent and the rest if it may be anointed with scent; if it may not, do so to its reflection in a mirror.

Wind a garland of flowers about its crown, offer water for welcoming before it, and encircle it all around with lamps. "One should fumigate it with incense, together with white and red incense, over which the all-purpose action mantra and the syllable hūṃ have been recited seven times": this is the dispelling of misfortune. Having done that, mix in a copper vessel the five nectars, namely curd, milk, butter, honey and sugar; and after that, likewise place in a copper vessel curd, milk, butter, cow dung and cow urine. Taking up bundles of dūrvā grass over which the all-purpose action mantra has been recited, and saying oṃ hūṃ trāṃ hrīḥ a, anoint it thoroughly, as before, according to whether it may or may not be anointed directly. Bathe it with fragrant water over which the all-purpose action mantra has been recited, and then scrub it with the powder of śirīṣa, udumbara, paḍa and parkadra kept in a bronze vessel. Saying oṃ hūṃ trāṃ hrīḥ a, anoint it with perfume kept in a bronze vessel and with melted butter, and scrub it once more. Anoint it with myrobalan kept in a bronze vessel and wash it; anoint it with turmeric kept in a bronze vessel and wash it; then anoint it with sandalwood, both śrīkhaṇḍa and candana, and whatever else has been gathered. Thinking that the Tathāgatas and the goddesses, invited by the rays of light from the seed syllable at one's heart, are bathing it with their vases, and preceded by auspicious songs, music and the like, bathe it with the water of the Vijaya vase and the others, poured into a conch shell or some other vessel, while reciting the verses that begin "Just as at the moment of birth."

Then wipe off the water on it with a soft cloth, and adorn it with garments and the like as far as one is able. With the rays of light from the seed syllable at one's heart, invite into the sky before one the wisdom being to be consecrated, along with the deities, the buddhas, bodhisattvas and the rest. Worship them properly, beginning with water for welcoming, and then, setting the left knee on the ground, ringing the bell with the left hand and holding the censer with the right, say:

oṃ hūṃ vajra jihvā
Blessed One, so-and-so, supreme vajra,
king of awareness, I bow to you and praise you.
Protector, whose very nature is loving,
I ask that the consecration be done through you.
Out of love for the disciples,
in order to worship all of you,
to increase the merit of beings,
and so that the awakening mind may arise,
I am devoted to this, Blessed One;
it is fitting that you grant your kindness.
Buddhas, turn your minds to me rightly,
and grant what is to be done for the circle of beings.
Bodhisattvas who abide in the fruit,
and whatever other deities of mantra there are,
gods and protectors of the world,
bhūtas, those who abide in perfect awakening,
those who delight in teaching beings,
and whatever others possess the divine eye:
I am so-and-so, the great vajra,
and I wish to perform the consecration of so-and-so;
therefore it is fitting
that you all come near.

Recite this three times. Having requested them to come near for the sake of the consecration and for the sake of the preliminary installation (adhivāsana), worship them properly, bow and praise them, and then send them away with oṃ vajra punarāgamanaya muḥ. Anoint thoroughly the heart, crown, throat and uṣṇīṣa of the bathed image or other object, and while reciting the all-purpose action mantra touch it three times with the right hand, and recite its own mantra just as it is. "One should also offer the torma to the protectors of the directions": this is the preliminary installation.

Then in the morning, having arranged everything as before on that same bathing platform, at an auspicious moment or the like, the master in the form of a vajra holder sits near, together with his consort. First he gives the torma, and after emptiness he visualizes the image or other object in the form of the pledge being. As before, he performs the bathing and the dispelling of misfortune, the anointing with the five nectars just described, and everything up to wiping it with water. Then, bringing it into the maṇḍala house, he sets it facing the maṇḍala, and, beginning with the blessing of the eyes and the rest, in the sequence for inviting the wisdom being just described, he recites three times from "Blessed One" up to "that you all come near." He causes the wisdom being to enter the pledge being, makes them of one taste, and then confers the empowerment.

Then he makes a request to the Tathāgatas of the ten directions and to the goddesses Locanā and the rest, invited by the rays of light from the seed syllable at his own heart, with this verse:

Just as the buddhas bestowed
the empowerment that is the source of qualities
on the Vajra Holder for the protection of beings,
so likewise bestow it upon this one.

He draws them into his own body by way of the path of Vairocana, enters into union with his consort, and makes them melt through great passion. They emerge by way of the vajra path, and with that melted substance he empowers the image or other object, which, having entered through his own mouth, has been placed in the lotus of the goddess; and it emerges from the lotus. Then he visualizes those beings endowed with hands, faces and the rest conferring empowerment, preceded by auspicious songs and the like, upon what has emerged from the lotus. Holding in the right hand a vajra together with a leafy sprig, he takes the water of the Vijaya vase and the others as the nectar of the awakening mind, and reciting the verses beginning "Just as at the moment of birth," and also oṃ sarba tathāgata abhiṣeka samaya śriye hūṃ svāhā and oṃ vajra udaka abhiṣiñca hūṃ, he confers the empowerment with firm and special conviction that it is the lord of the maṇḍala, the Blessed One Vajrasattva himself alone, who is empowering. This is the water empowerment. "With oṃ hūṃ trāṃ hrīḥ a one should place the crown upon it": this is the crown empowerment. As before, in every case there is special conviction in the Blessed One alone. At this same point a silk ribbon is bound on the forehead of the image or other object. "With the verse that begins 'Today,' one should have it hold the vajra": this is the vajra empowerment. "Reciting oṃ vajra adhipati stvaṃ abhiṣiñcami tiṣṭha vajra samaya stvaṃ, one should have it hold the bell": this is the bell empowerment.

"Reciting oṃ vajrasatva stvaṃ abhiṣiñcami vajra nāma abhiṣekataḥ and oṃ vajrasatva, one should give it a name": this is the name empowerment. "Visualizing the image or other object embracing the wisdom consort with its two hands, which hold the vajra and the vajra bell, one empowers it by the rite of the water empowerment; seeing that it has been sealed by the lord of the family, one causes the remaining Tathāgatas and the rest to enter into it, and one should bless it with the mantra oṃ supratiṣṭha vajre svāhā": this is the master empowerment. "The body of the lord of the family, the Lord of the Wheel, that is, Vajrasattva, together with the host of Tathāgatas, Vairocana and the rest, with their consorts, invited by the rays of light from the seed syllable at one's own heart, enter by way of Vairocana, melt, and are savored with great bliss; and one should imagine the substance of the awakening mind, arisen from the vajra and the lotus, entering the mouth of the image or other object": this is the secret empowerment. "Then the goddess bestowed by that Vajrasattva and the deities of the image or other object enter into union, and one should have special conviction that this is of the nature of innate joy": this is the fourth empowerment.

Then, with the heart mantra of the deity, offer garments and the other offerings; give it the pledge substances and bezoar as well, and show it a mirror with the mantra oṃ vajrasatva āḥ. Then, for the sake of the patron and the others and for the pacification or other result that is desired, with a golden probe of no less than a māṣaka in weight, smeared with butter and honey kept in a silver vessel, reciting oṃ cakṣu cakṣu samanta cakṣura viśodhane svāhā, apply the eye ointment with the thought of opening its eyes. Then one should be convinced that it gazes in the supreme way for the pacification and other aims of the patron and the others.

Then, on the fire of the fire-offering hearth, or if there is none, elsewhere in a vessel set on a stand, mix milk, unbroken rice, butter, honey and sugar, and prepare a milk porridge while reciting the mantra of one's own lord together with its completing syllables. Consecrating it while reciting oṃ dibyanne samādhi dhyana prīṇane svāhā, have the image or other object partake of it, directly or in its reflection, and offer it water for rinsing the mouth and betel as well. Make it hear the pledge with oṃ hūṃ trāṃ hrīḥ a, present flowers and the like, and with palms joined recite the verses beginning "Just as all the perfect buddhas," and request it to remain for a long time. With the right hand in the playful vajra gesture, circle three times the lower, middle and upper parts of the image or other object, and praise it with the eight-line mantra and the verses beginning "Akṣobhya." Recite three times the hundred-syllable mantra that begins oṃ vajrasatva, and say:

Because things were not obtained and not fully known,
because of lack of ability, whatever was incomplete,
and whatever else has been done,
you whose nature is love should forgive it all.
Perfect buddhas and deities,
their heirs, Brahmā and the rest:
whatever in the rite for gods and nāgas
was incomplete and so on, you should forgive.
Protect the image for a long time
from earth, water, fire and wind.
For the patron bring about pacification, increase,
and auspiciousness in every way.
Grant this as well to his children and the others, to the disciples,
and to us;
and though you depart to the buddha realms,
we pray that you will come again.

Having made this request three times, offer the torma for the directions and the torma for all the bhūtas. The patron, too, makes offerings, prostrations and so on to the image or other object, and should then please the master with a fee and the like. This is the rite of consecration.

Alternatively, having dissolved the image or other object into emptiness, in an instant generate the form of the pledge being of perfect awakening and bless its eyes and the rest. With the rays of light from the seed syllable at one's own heart, invite the wisdom being and absorb it within. Empower it with the Tathāgatas and others invited in just the same way and with the water of one's own vase, worship it properly, and recite its mantra one hundred and eight times. By this too one should consecrate the image or other object.

As for a rosary, immediately after emptiness visualize it as Speech Vajra and make it one with the wisdom being. Imagine that this transforms into the form of the rosary, sprinkle it with the water of the vase to purify it, worship it properly, and recite the heart mantra of Speech Vajra one hundred and eight times. This is the brief consecration of a rosary.

If one asks how the Blessed One comes to abide in an image that has not been consecrated, the text says "how not consecrated" and so forth. "Requests" means making requests. "Faith" means "of faith." "Will do" stands for "having done." "The object of worship and the worship are equal" means that the object of worship and the worship become equal.

This is the explanation of the twenty-second chapter.

To teach what was asked about the fire offering, the text says "Then" and so forth. "Sixteen finger-widths": a hearth of sixteen finger-widths or of eighteen finger-widths is for increase. One of twenty finger-widths is for pacification, as in "It pacifies epidemic diseases," since it serves to pacify epidemic disease. "These are the rules for the hearth" means this is the rule. "The measures of the substances to be burned" means the kindling sticks and the rest, in proportion to the measure of the hearth. "In accord with the rite, form and fruit" means the color and the emblem. "Of three parts of the breadth" means that it should be bordered with two parts of the measure across the hearth. "This is the hearth" means this applies to all the hearths. "By an eighth part" means by an eighth part of the mouth of the hearth. "In the center of the hearth, especially, it is marked with vajras": the vajras are by their very nature the Tathāgatas. The meaning is that the emblems should be arranged according to the particular rite. Thus in the hearth for fierce rites there is a wrathful vajra resting on a black lotus. In the hearth for increase there is a jewel marked with a vajra, resting on the hub of an eight-spoked wheel. In the hearth for subjugation there is a vajra resting on a red lotus. In the hearth for pacification there is a peaceful vajra resting on a white lotus.

In the hearth for all rites there is a lotus encircled by a vajra garland. On the petals of the directions are the emblems of the four Tathāgatas. On the anthers are emblems in accord with the rite. Their size is whatever looks beautiful. "A lotus at one's own heart" means the syllable hūṃ resting on a lotus at the heart of one's own form as Vajrasattva. "The melted form arising from the three syllables" is the oral instruction for the fire offering. The rest is easy to understand.

Here is the condensed meaning of this. Endowed with the yoga of two-armed Cakrasaṃvara, and having first given the torma, meditate that the ground within the hearth to be dug is empty. Press it with the hand and recite the mantra of the Lord of the Wheel one hundred and eight times; then dig there a hearth in accord with the rite, having the characteristics described for the hearths of all the rites. Smear it with earth and cow dung mixed together, as befits the rite, likewise sprinkle it with scented water, and scatter flowers.

Then, provided with kindling sticks, firewood and the like in accord with the rite, the master, first whirling the vajra and ringing the bell, gives the torma and performs the rite of protection. With the mantra of Khaṇḍarohā he kindles the fire from a burning butter lamp, and with water over which that mantra has been recited, taken up on the tips of kuśa grass, he sprinkles it for purification. Circling clockwise for pacification and counterclockwise for fierce rites, he places the fire in the hearth together with a clean tuft of dry grass, free of smoke, though for fierce rites it is with smoke. He sets it ablaze with the syllable hūṃ and a fan, and makes it blaze up with butter in a ladle over which the heart mantra of the family has been recited seven times; for fierce rites he uses pungent substances and sesame oil.

Then, reciting oṃ vajrasatva, he lays fresh kuśa grass, green, spotless, untorn and unbroken, two blades at a time on the platform, with the tips pointing east, south, west and north. With other blades, not too long and shaped like a cow's ear, he surrounds the hearth, with the tips pointing east, north, west and south for pacification, increase, subjugation and fierce rites respectively. East and the rest are reckoned according to the direction the practitioner faces.

Then he visualizes the hearth, of the nature of the deity, arisen from the seed syllable hūṃ. In its center is emptiness, and from raṃ arises a maṇḍala of fire. Upon it, resting on a lotus and a sun, is the fire god, arisen from the syllable raṃ: short, with a hanging belly, three faces and six arms, each face with three eyes. With his two principal hands he embraces a wisdom consort of his own radiance; of the remaining hands, the right ones show the gesture of fearlessness and hold a rosary, and the left ones hold a staff and a water pot. He has a beard and a topknot of matted locks, he is endowed with a garland of rays of light of consciousness, and he is sealed by the lord of the family. His body color and ornaments accord with the rite. When all his limbs and his principal face are white, his right and left faces are black and red. When they are yellow, red or green, the side faces are blue and white. When they are blue, the side faces are white and red. At his heart one visualizes the pledge being blessed by the maṇḍala of fire with its seed syllable raṃ. With the hook of light rays from the seed syllable at its heart, one should invite the wisdom being, the fire god in the likeness of the pledge being, into the sky in the direction of Agni, saying:

oṃ Come here, come here, great being,
divine sage, supreme brahmin;
in order to partake of the food of the burnt offering,
please take your seat near to us here.

oṃ agneye ādībya ādībya āviśa āviśa mahāśriye habya kabya vahanāya svāhā

Having invited him before one in this way, and beginning with water for welcoming, one should summon him with the syllables jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ, absorb him into the pledge being, bind him, and please him.

Then on the left side, in vessels made of gold or the like, or if there are none, in leaf cups, place flowers in accord with the rite and fine perfume together with the five nectars: in particular, for pacification, with sesame, parched rice, barley and milk, and for increase, with sesame and curd. Set out these three vessels, over which the three syllables, the mantra of the Lord of the Wheel and the all-purpose action mantra have each been recited seven times, and in front of them set three receiving bowls. For the purificatory sprinkling, take water from the vessel of water for rinsing the mouth with a bundle of kuśa grass, and, with the mantra oṃ āḥ hrīḥ pravara satkāra prokṣaṇaṃ pratīccha hūṃ svāhā, circle the hand around with a graceful flourish, and with the thought of offering the sprinkling to his body, offer it three times into a receiving bowl. From that same vessel take water together with flowers with the fingers of the fist turned downward, extend the little finger, ring finger, middle finger and index finger in turn, and with the thought of washing his hands or face, offer it three times into that same bowl, putting āñcamanaṃ in place of prokṣaṇaṃ in the mantra just given. With this same mantra one should also offer water for welcoming and water for the feet three times each, inserting the words arghaṃ and pādyaṃ. From the vessel of water for welcoming, take water together with flowers in the vajra-cupped hands, and with the thought of offering it before him, offer it into the second receiving bowl: this is the water for welcoming. From the vessel of water for the feet, take water together with flowers with the index finger and thumb of the fist turned upward, circle it around with a graceful flourish, extend the index finger, middle finger, ring finger and little finger in turn, and with the thought of offering it to his feet, offer it into the third receiving bowl: this is the water for the feet. At every point where water for welcoming and the rest are offered, this same procedure applies.

Now one proclaims the pledge three times with oṃ vajra anala mahābhūta jvalaya sarba bhasmiṃ kuru sarba duṣṭaṃ hūṃ phaṭ dṛśya jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ samaya stvaṃ samayo haṃ. Made of gold or the like, or of sacrificial wood, its handle is twenty-four thumb-widths of a full-grown man in length, and of a thickness that can be grasped by a fist holding a vajra. On top of the handle is a square of four finger-widths, two finger-widths deep, marked in its center with a five-pronged vajra of two finger-widths, and marked on the four sides above the hollow with a vajra garland one finger-width wide.

Above the square it is marked with a vajra of four finger-widths and has a depth of the measure of the little finger; it bears the design of lotus petals and has a hole the size of the little finger through which the essence drips. Below the handle there is a jewel of three finger-widths, and below that a vajra of three finger-widths. This is filled with butter and held facing upward in the left hand, in the gesture of supreme awakening. The one held facing downward in the right hand is the pouring ladle: its handle is about a cubit long and half as thick as the handle of the filling ladle. The upper part of the handle is shaped like a lotus petal, two finger-widths wide and one finger-width deep, and is marked with a five-pronged vajra. The lower part has a vajra of three finger-widths and is marked with a jewel of three finger-widths. The filling ladle faces upward and is filled with butter. For fierce rites, however, both are filled with poison and pungent melted butter and are held the other way round.

On the mouths of the filling ladle and the pouring ladle place the seed syllable of one's chosen deity, interspersed within the three syllables and accompanied by rays of light, and set raṃ in the mouth of the fire god. With the mouth of the pouring ladle covering the mouth of the filling ladle, place the hands between the knees, though for fierce rites outside them, and circle them around, clockwise for fierce rites and counterclockwise for pacification. With the mouth slightly open, and for fierce rites wide open, one should give three or seven full-ladle offerings with the mantra that begins oṃ agneye ādipya. oṃ agneye svāhā is the mantra for the other burnt offerings. When the full-ladle offering is made, the signs of virtue and nonvirtue should be recognized from the color of the fire and the other indications, as they have been taught. If there are signs of nonvirtue, one should perform for the fire the purificatory sprinkling, the protection, and seven burnt offerings of butter with the mantra of Khaṇḍarohā, and then with the mantra of the fire god as well. Then one should again perform the purificatory sprinkling with the all-purpose action mantra and offer three full-ladle offerings with the mantra of the fire god.

First of all, wash the kindling sticks and the rest thoroughly with the mantra oṃ svāhā. Then one should purify the kindling sticks with oṃ āḥ svāhā, the grains with oṃ jrīṃ svāhā, the other substances with oṃ kuru kuru svāhā, and the butter with oṃ śrī svāhā. Then, into the blazing fire, offer in order the kindling sticks, butter, sesame, dūrvā grass, rice and curd-rice, each with its own mantra.

For pacification, the kindling sticks are from milky trees such as palāśa, udumbara, the bodhi tree, kāśmarī, mango, kadamba, śamī, plakṣa and vaṭa, taken from the upper part of the tree, with a pair of leaves at the tip, not crooked, cut evenly, still moist, and about as thick as the little finger. Their two ends are dipped in honey, milk and butter, and they are thoroughly purified with nectar and scented water. Holding them with the ring finger and the thumb, one should offer each one tip first into the fire. Kuśa grass, mango leaves and dūrvā grass are offered joined in pairs. Rice, milk, sesame, barley and curd, priyaṅgu, sandalwood and parched rice are offered as they are obtained.

For increase, the kindling sticks are of those same trees but taken from the middle part. They are sprinkled with yellow scented water containing nectar and dipped in curd, milk, butter and the three sweet substances. Also offered are fine food, black sesame mixed with curd and honey, curd-rice, śatapuṣpa dipped in butter, bilva fruit, kharma rice, lotus, nāgakeśara and karṇikāra flowers, and grains.

For subjugation, one uses campaka, aśoka, pantaga, nāgakeśara, kesara, lakuca and priyaṅgu, together with kindling sticks, fruits and flowers having the characteristics described for pacification, as they are obtained.

For fierce rites, the kindling sticks are from hot, thorny and bitter trees such as neem and khadira, taken from the root part, dipped in poison, blood and pungent mustard oil, and sharp at both ends. One uses pungent mustard oil, blood, white mustard seed, ske tshe mustard, neem leaves and the like, as they are obtained; or else one makes an effigy of the enemy from ashes, charcoal, chaff and the like, dips it in poison and the rest, and, beginning from the little toe of the left foot, cuts it with a sharp blade and offers it into the fire.

For all rites, the kindling sticks are like those for pacification, together with fresh ones of khadira, arka, kaṅkata and apāmārga, sprinkled with fragrant water containing nectar, with both ends dipped in oil or the like as suits the rite, about a span in length. Also offered are the butter, milk and the rest taught for pacification and increase, guggulu mixed with butter, or white incense, and whatever little food one eats oneself, all of it.

As for firewood: sacrificial woods and the dung of pasture cows are for pacification. The same, leaving out the dung of pasture cows, is for increase. Wood of neem and myrobalan trees, together with their flowers, is for subjugation. Wood from thorny trees and the like, wood from a charnel ground, and the dry dung of donkeys and other animals are for fierce rites. The kindling sticks, however, are offered into his radiance; foods such as curd-rice, and fruits, into his hands; drinks into his mouth; incense and lamps before him; and flowers on his crown. One should offer them both to the fire and to one's own chosen deity.

After that, give the fire god water for rinsing the mouth and the purificatory sprinkling. At his heart, upon a lotus, generate the Blessed One complete just as he is, together with his retinue and in union, or else alone, with body color and the rest in accord with the rite. Imagine that the emanated bodies and the deities of the maṇḍala accomplish the desired aims; oneself, too, being endowed with that yoga, purify the kindling sticks and the rest with the syllable hūṃ and the mantra of Khaṇḍarohā. Invite the wisdom being, and, beginning as before with the purificatory sprinkling and the rest, cause it to enter the pledge being. Imagining everything up to the completion of the empowerment, one should perform the burnt offerings as desired, with the heart mantra or whatever mantra is desired, in the same sequence as before, beginning with the full-ladle offering.

Then, at the end, offer three, seven, or twenty-one burnt offerings of ghee with the mantras of the Lord of the Wheel and the rest alone, and offer the full-ladle offering as well; then one should offer the guest water and the rest while reciting the heart mantra. Then, having purified them, offer water to rinse the mouth, new garments, and betel without lime, though for the fierce rite betel with lime; then worship them properly, praise them, prostrate, offer guest water, join the palms at the heart, and say:

Whatever slight wrong I have done,
I whose mind is deluded,
since you are the refuge of embodied beings,
O Protector, please forgive it.
O Lord, because I lack the ability,
have not obtained, and do not fully know,
whatever actions have been done here,
it is fitting that all of these be forgiven.

Having thus asked for forbearance, in order to pacify the faults of incompleteness and the like, and also for the desired accomplishment, recite the hundred-syllable mantra. Reciting the verse that begins "oṃ, by you, for sentient beings," cause the deities to depart. Again properly worship the fire god dwelling in the hearth, offer the remaining substances as burnt offerings, and cause him to depart as before. In this way, on each day, one should perform it at the times stated for the pacifying rite and the others, and also in three or four sessions. That is what is meant.

When performing the fire offering for the sake of another, hold the right thumb of the disciple who has offered the fee with one's left little finger and have him circle the hearth three times clockwise. Circling the ladle around his head, give seven burnt offerings, and then offer the full-ladle offering. Tie on his hand a protection cord over which the all-purpose mantra has been recited, and offer the purificatory torma. Then, following the procedure taught in the section on consecration, and not omitting the caru, tie around his neck a garland of flowers anointed with perfume in keeping with the rite. Having fully prepared the porridge, which is of the nature of nectar and arisen from the seed hūṃ, say:

This is an offering to the Tathāgata;
eaten, it utterly destroys evil.

Then offer it to the gods, the world protectors, and the rest. The patron and oneself should also eat of it, and it should be shared with any who want it. After that, extinguish the fire: for the pacifying rite with milk, for the rite of increase with yellow water, for the rite of subjugation with red milk, and for the fierce rite with black liquid. Carry the ashes and the rest from the fire-offering hearth in the all-purpose vase, and send them off into the current of a flowing river. That is what is meant.

Having taught the outer fire offering, in order to show the inner fire offering it says, "The world," and so on; this is easy to understand. This is the explanation of the twenty-third chapter.

In order to answer the question, "How do the mantras become accomplished?" it says, "Then," and so on. "The state of various accomplishments" means that various accomplishments are properly present: it is just as the results whose nature is various actions arise for whichever beings. "By an approach of ten thousand" means by the yoga of one's own chosen deity. "Holding the liṅga" means the liṅga of the Great God (Śiva); some say it means one's own liṅga. For the mantras beginning "oṃ ca muṇḍa," the preliminary approach is just the same. For the mantras beginning "oṃ vajra" it is likewise; in all of these, for mantras for which no special preliminary approach is stated, the sequence of preliminary approach is just that.

"Mixed" means the remainder. "Nāga flower" means the flower of the nāga tree. "With the syllables yaṃ" means with wind maṇḍalas. "A leaf moved by the wind" means one carried off by a whirlwind, it is said. The meaning is this: take a leaf carried off by the wind, and on it, with poison and the like, using a pen made of a crow's feather, write inside the letter ya: "hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ so-and-so ucchaṭaya phaṭ phaṭ phaṭ." Write this on two leaves and place them face to face. At night, at a shrine with a single liṅga, recite the mantra, and send it off into a whirlwind or a violent river; the enemy will be driven away. For the practice beginning "with cow dung that has not fallen to the ground," it is this same mantra. Some say that the practices beginning from "in the corner of the wind" use the heart mantra and the near-heart mantra of the Blessed One (the Buddha). "Temple" means at a shrine with a single liṅga. "Of all" means: the words to be supplied are "of all, accomplishment comes from devotion to the guru"; the intent is that without the guru's instructions there is no accomplishment.

"Two" means the white and the red; both are to be counted as one. So too with the three hot spices and the three fruits. "Flower tree and turmeric" means the flower tree and tree turmeric. "By the sun" means for going toward the sun. "Fasting observance" means by the poṣadha. This is the explanation of the twenty-fourth chapter.

In order to answer the question, "How, too, should the elixir be regarded?" it says, "Then," and so on. "It becomes the defining mark of the method for the body" means that it becomes the method for making the body firm. "Moon" and so on is the brief presentation. Here, the moon is Amitābha; storax is Ratnasambhava; musk is Akṣobhya; medicine is Amoghasiddhi; and yellow sandalwood is Vairocana. "Each" means that even one alone will do. "Five" means properly obtained from the five classes endowed with the pledges and so on.

"First and last": here the instruction on its potency is this. In a tube made of neem wood, or a tube made of bamboo, make two holes so as to divide it into three parts. Of Akṣobhya that has cooled, discard the upper part and take the middle part. In the bamboo tube, put powder of ketaka branches; then the part of fatty fluid will be above it, the part of salt below, and the pure part in between. "Pacifies" means it pacifies the planets and the rest. "Always becoming equal in four" means always making one youthful. "Drinking the moon in its course": the words to be supplied are "one should drink the moon with the moon." Therefore "course" means coursing in the body. If one asks when, it says, "The moon," and so on: the moon alone, or else together with other substances. That very point it states with "These," and so on. "Dark" means what is not red. "Stains and the stainless entirely removed" means free of the parts of fatty fluid and salt.

"Excellent," and so on: one should take what has become so by its own nature. This is the detailed explanation of the brief presentation. The "lump" is the body, and its "binding" is making it firm. The procedure for the medicines is taught with "Fish," and so on. "Properly endowed" means having made it endowed. "Of that" means "by that." "The five" are the five substances themselves. "Understanding" means that purification, understanding, and the like will come first.

Here the condensed meaning is this. In some place free of harm, build a hut. Take as one's companion in the practice a single disciple endowed with faith and supreme devotion, and give him gifts and a fee. Oneself enter the hut, please one's own deity with tormas and offerings, overcome the others, and purify one's own body for as long as seven years.

The sequence of purification is as follows. On an auspicious day, having eaten a little molasses, one should drink a decoction of the three fruits together with salt and powder of the fruit of the lac tree; worms will fall out and impurities will be cleansed. Then mix together juice of ketaka branches, warm fatty fluid, hilamoci, and rock salt and drink it; heat oneself in the sun and press the body, and one will be free of lice. Drink those same things together with juice of hilamuci and press the body in just the same way, and the body will be free of salt. If one drinks juice of hilamuci with rock salt and stays in the shade, one will be free of bile. Mix juice of kataka branches, juice of telakuca, and cow's milk in equal parts and drink it, and one will be free of fatty fluid. Drink juice of kuṣmāṇḍa branches with rock salt, and one will be free of eye disease. If honey is poured into the ears, one will be free of phlegm. These applications too should be spaced one or two days apart, and each should be applied twice. Then, each day, one should drink musk kept in a stone vessel together with the three fruits. One should eat food free of fish, meat, alcohol, milk, and sour things, and should also give up intercourse with women. Then, at the three vajras, one should rely on a single portion of the five nectars, or on all of them, according to the rite. After that, giving up the medicine, one should remain for three years, eating only at the times for medicine and engaging in the activities of conduct.

The rubbing powder is taught. "Buddha" means cakramardaka. "Akara" means turmeric. "Balguje" means somarāja. "Musk" means the part of fatty fluid. With sandalwood, and as a dry powder. "Car" means spikenard. Grind these substances with musk and use them as a rubbing powder.

If one is unable to perform the procedure of the heating practice, the procedure of the elixir is taught: "The three fruits," and so on. In the procedure of the elixir, one need not entirely avoid cold, wind, and the like. Sour leaves, alcohol, and intercourse with women should be avoided. Of these, the fourth application is this: take the central part of the musk by the procedure just as it was taught, mix it with powders of the three fruits and sandalwood, and drink it. The three fruits, sandalwood powder, and musk should be purified and placed in an iron vessel. Having poured musk into a stone vessel and put powder of the three fruits on top, one should drink it at the proper times. These too should be set out in the morning and taken when one session has passed. One should eat one measure each of the three fruits and sandalwood powder every day. One should practice whichever of these four applications one likes. This is the explanation of the twenty-fifth chapter.

In order to teach what was asked about liquor, it says, "Then," and so on. "Not stated elsewhere": it is not that it is not stated here; "elsewhere" means in the action tantras and the like. "Wisdom vajra" means Vajrasattva. The sphere of space is the sphere of phenomena (dharmadhātu). Mandāra and the ocean of milk are examples based simply on what is well known in the world. Or else wisdom vajra and sphere of space are other conventional terms. "On nectar" is the locative case expressing reason. "Arising from Mandāra" means flowing down from Mandāra.
