The second offering verse reads gzungs ("dhāraṇī"), evidently for gzung ("the grasped"), paired with 'dzin pa ("grasper"); translated accordingly. The repeated formula "the one whose ... it is" renders the commentary's standard analysis of compounds as possessive (bahuvrīhi). "The syllable yaṃ is restrictive" renders a grammatical gloss on the root verse that is not fully clear without the Sanskrit. The karmavajra is the ritual assistant who serves the feast.  "The offering syllable" renders phud yig, literally "first-portion syllable"; its exact identity is not stated. "Examining silk" (dar dpyad) is a literal rendering of the name of a hand sign whose Sanskrit original is not certain. The glosses "'Ḍākinī' is in the first case" and "'From the left' stands for 'of one born'" are grammatical remarks on the root text whose point depends on the Sanskrit wording.  The list of heroes is introduced with a feminine form ("heroines") in the Tibetan, but Khaṇḍakapāla is the first of the male heroes of the twenty-four places, so "heroes" is given. Pūllira is the Tibetan spelling of the first of the twenty-four places, usually Pullīramalaya. "So-and-so" and "Devadatta" stand for the name of the person for whom the rite is performed. In the fever rite the verb read as "strokes" is written bya ba, evidently for byi ba, "to wipe, sweep down." Gīvaṃ is probably gorocanā (bezoar), a standard pigment for such rites. "In the manner of a vidyā" presumably means worn as a protective amulet.  "So-and-so" and "Devadatta" stand for the name of the person targeted by the rite. "Upon a woman's lotus" is literal; it may mean the wheels are drawn within a lotus figure representing the female organ. In "the middle row running in each of the two directions," the Tibetan reads literally "the middle of three rows"; the count of thirteen empty squares confirms that the central cross of the seven-by-seven grid is meant. In the immobilizing rite the Tibetan says the mantra lines on the outer rim have their heads pointing "nad du," read here as nang du, "inward."  "Devadatta," "Yajñadatta" and "so-and-so" stand for the names of the persons targeted by the rite. "The transference from the body" renders a terse phrase; the point is that only a yogin able to transfer the victim's consciousness to a higher rebirth may perform the rite of killing. The verse quoted from the Hevajra (King of Tantras in Two Examinations) is translated as it stands here.  "The four, blue, green, red and yellow": the Tibetan does not name what these four are; in Cakrasaṃvara sādhanas they are the four wrathful protectors of the directions arising from the four sumbha mantras. Nāda is the subtle sound at the end of a seed syllable.  "Bhairaṃpa" is the Tibetan spelling of the name of the wind circle that forms the base of the visualized support, elsewhere vairambha; it is kept as written. The goddesses of the elements, Pātanī and the rest, follow the Saṃvarodaya's list beginning with Pātanī. "The goddess Rasanā" renders ལྷ་མོ་རོ་མ, the goddess of the right channel. pū ja are the seed syllables of the first of the twenty-four sacred places, Pullīramalaya and Jālandhara.  The passage first sets out an opponent's case against the generation stage (from reasoning and scripture), then three views on why deity forms are taught, and finally the author's own reply in the last paragraph. Here བུམ་པ is the ordinary "pot" of logical examples, not the zodiac sign named earlier. In the Hevajra verse, འདུས་པ is rendered "assembly" (samāja) as the verse's own word rather than as the title Guhyasamāja. འཕགས་པའི་ཞབས (Āryapāda) most likely refers to Nāgārjuna, but the text gives only the epithet.  Here, as in the previous piece, བུམ་པ is the "pot" of logical examples, not the zodiac sign. The Blessed Lady refers to the Perfection of Wisdom scripture. In the Hevajra verse, ཀུན་དུ་རུ (kunduru) is rendered "union," its sense in this tantra. The Blessed One's reply to Vajragarbha continues in the next piece, so the rendering of its last line ("by the very force of faith this has gone astray") is provisional.  The work cited from Āryadevapāda as the Blending of Sūtra and Tantra is probably his Caryāmelāpakapradīpa, whose question-and-answer exchange with the vajra master matches this passage. ཐ་ག་པ ("the weaver") is taken as the siddha Tantipa, whose name means weaver; this identification is not certain. "Thirty-third chapter" refers to a chapter of the root tantra, not the Heaven of the Thirty-Three. In the Avikalpapraveśa verse, དབང་ཆེན is kept as "Mahendra" for consistency, though here it may be an epithet ("great lord") rather than a name.  The Source of the Radiant Vajra (རབ་ཏུ་སྣང་བའི་རྡོ་རྗེ་འབྱུང་བ) is translated by meaning; its Sanskrit title is not identified with confidence. The Ornament of the Vajra Essence is likely the Vajrahṛdayālaṃkāra tantra. The final section comments word by word on the root tantra's chapter on worshipping maidens, so quoted words are the root text's lemmas; the note on "not properly worshipped" refers to the Sanskrit negative prefix a- having been elided in the root text's reading.  This section glosses the root tantra word by word, so the quoted words are the root text's lemmas and some glosses are terse. "The act declines" is glossed with a phrase whose exact sense is unclear; it has been rendered literally as "the commitment that must be guarded has become exposed." "Fierce one" (གཏུམ་པོ) is one of the skull types in the root text; it may stand for the caṇḍāla class, but this is not certain.  "Vairocana and the rest" are the coded names of the five nectars, and "go, ku and the rest" are the coded syllables of the five meats (go for cow, ku for dog, and so on); "the first flower" refers to first menstrual blood. "On the tenth day" renders bcu pa la, which could also be read as "for the tenth" substance; the sense is not fully certain. The Tibetan 'gyur in the root verses ("four units," "eight units," "two units") is glossed by the commentator as a minor part (cha chung), a quarter of the door width, and is rendered "unit" throughout.  The month names (Khrums, Mgo, Dbo, Snron) are rendered by their usual Indian equivalents Bhādrapada, Mārgaśīrṣa, Phālguna and Jyeṣṭha, which fall three months apart, matching the nāga's three-month turns. The instruction both to avoid two or three squares "at the belly" and then to dig "at the belly" is rendered as written; the first may refer to a margin around the belly area. "Watches of the day" renders dbyu gu, an Indian time unit (daṇḍa).  "White mustard" renders don grub (Skt. siddhārtha), a common name for white mustard seed in ritual texts. The peg measurements are given as the Tibetan states them; the clause on thickness is terse and its exact sense is not certain.  The plant names are given in their Sanskrit forms as the Tibetan transcribes them. "Without cracks" renders rtsa ba med pa, whose sense here is not entirely certain.  Agni's, Nairṛti's, Vāyu's and Īśāna's corners are the southeast, southwest, northwest and northeast. The phrase rendered "up to the thumb" (sa mthe bor gyi rta bar) is obscure in the Tibetan as it stands.  The verse on the archway uses Indian symbolic number words: "wealth" (vasu) for eight, "sun" for twelve, "victor" (jina) for twenty-four, "ocean" for four, and "arrow" for five. They are rendered here as numerals; the units in which the eleven bands are counted are not stated in this piece. "With a half" (Skt. sārdha) is taken as "and a half". The verse measurements are highly compressed and their precise geometry is uncertain. "Horns" above the wheel is a literal rendering of the Tibetan.  "Bakuli" is left as a Sanskrit loanword for an architectural molding of the archway; its exact form is uncertain. The word written as "maṅ yab" in the passage on banners is taken as a slip for "parapet" (mda' yab), which fits the context. "Sounds of intense joy" (rab tu dga' ba'i sgra) is taken here as the sounds of bliss in union, not as a name. In the final sentence, "flower" and "awakening mind" are the usual tantric code for the consort's and the practitioner's sexual fluids.  The rite called "ritual touching" here is the purificatory touching of the senses (upasparśana); the text mentions no water. Stobs kyi rgyu is taken as the standard Tibetan rendering of the nāga king Karkoṭa, which matches the mantra that follows.  "Gentle one" renders mnyes gshin, the master's form of address to the disciple (probably Skt. saumya).  In the hymn, "great lord" (dbang phyug chen po, Maheśvara) is an epithet of the central vajra deity, not a reference to Śiva. "Kuṇḍalin" is the coiled one, Amṛtakuṇḍalin, whose mantra is used for protection. The seventh line of the hymn, rendered "beyond permanence" (rtag med pa), is uncertain.  "Until the disciple is filled" renders a terse phrase (literally "together with filling"), which most likely refers to reciting the descent mantra until the disciple is pervaded by the deity. The Tibetan "sun ngang" before "moon" is read as "sun and moon," an evident copyist's slip. The "five lamps" are the five meats of the ritual substances.  The lists give elements for the secret names conferred in the name empowerment. Since most are descriptive words rather than established deity names, they are rendered by their meaning; several of the women's names (Drawn-Face, Coming, Arising, Hand) are uncertain literal renderings.  As in the preceding lists, the women's names are mostly descriptive name-elements and are rendered by meaning; "Consoling" (literally "giving breath") and "Bliss Wonder" are uncertain. The line "Having filled the moon-like face" (literally "the face of the hare-marked one") is obscure in context.  The rasanā (literally "taster") is here probably the tongue rather than the right channel of the subtle body; the sentence is terse. The closing verse on the four empowerments is quoted elliptically, and its last line ("then, for that very one, that is here") is obscure.  The syllable at the sexual organ is spelled in the Tibetan with an unusual subjoined letter and is given as written. The figure for the Age of Perfection is given as the text states it (1,720,000). The phrase rendered "reckoned by tenths, together with the measure of the junctions at their boundaries" is terse; it seems to refer to the junction periods (sandhyā) of each age.  "White mustard seed" renders དོན་གྲུབ (siddhārtha), its usual ritual sense. The sentence about the patron holding the end of the garland and having the image "anointed as one who has beheld the maṇḍala" is syntactically compressed; the rendering follows the most natural reading. ཉ་ལྕིབས is taken as "fish scales"; the exact substance is uncertain. The second quoted verse closely resembles a well-known verse on the superiority of the mantra vehicle, but here its third line reads "great wisdom," and it is translated as it stands.  The four tree powders are given as spelled; paḍa and parkadra probably stand for vaṭa (banyan) and parkaṭī, two of the usual trees with milky sap, but this is not certain. "Bronze vessel" renders འཁར་བའི་སྣོད (bell metal). ཆེ་གེ is the placeholder "so-and-so," where the names of the deity, the officiant and the object are inserted. "The path of Vairocana" is the crown aperture and "the vajra path" the central channel's lower opening, in the usual sexual-yogic visualization of the secret empowerment applied here to the image.  "Completing syllables" renders ཁེངས་པ, apparently the added syllables that fill out the deity's mantra; the sense is not certain. "A vessel set on a stand" renders the unclear word མནྗི. In the explanation of "three parts of the breadth," the commentary says the border is two parts of the hearth's width as the text stands. Speech Vajra renders གསུང་རྡོ་རྗེ (Vāgvajra).  The last paragraph describes the offering ladle, whose name falls in the next piece; the Tibetan here gives its specifications without naming it. རིལ་པ, held by the fire god, is taken as a water pot. The phrase "garland of rays of light of consciousness" follows the Tibetan literally.  The Tibetan has མ་སྐྱེས་མའི་སྔགས, read as a slip for དུམ་སྐྱེས་མའི་སྔགས, the mantra of Khaṇḍarohā, which is prescribed for the same purpose in the preceding piece. The directions of circling (clockwise for fierce rites, counterclockwise for pacification) are given as the text has them, though they reverse what was said earlier for kindling the fire. "Pantaga" and "kharma rice" are given as spelled; the plants are not securely identified. "Offered into his radiance" renders འོད་ལའོ literally.  The verse beginning "oṃ, by you, for sentient beings" is the standard dismissal verse (Skt. oṃ kṛto vaḥ sarvasattvārthaḥ), here partly rendered in Tibetan. In the fourfold rites, dbang is the rite of subjugation, not empowerment. In the twenty-fourth-chapter comments, liṅga refers to Śiva's liṅga and liṅga shrines, not to an effigy. The gloss "by the sun means for going toward the sun" is obscure and is rendered literally. "Moon," "musk," and the other substances equated with the five buddhas are coded names for bodily substances in the elixir practice. The line glossed as "always making one youthful" reads bzhi mnyam ("equal in four"), rendered as written.  The passage gives a rejuvenation regimen whose plant names (hilamoci or hilamuci, telakuca, kataka beside ketaka, cakramardaka, somarāja, balguje) are partly transliterations that cannot all be identified with certainty; they are given as written. "Overcome the others" renders gzhan thub par byas literally. Drang ba ("straight") in the elixir section is read as grang ba ("cold"), which fits the list with wind. Kānta is taken as iron (kāntaloha). Shig is taken in its ordinary sense, lice. Zho is a small unit of weight, rendered "one measure." The genders mentioned at the end are the grammatical masculine, feminine and neuter of the Sanskrit terms for bliss and the awakening mind.  "Principal preparation" renders gtso bo in "gtso bo'i don du" and "gtso bo'i cho ga"; it may be a slip for bco bo ("brewing"), the word used later in "the procedure for brewing." Ananta here begins the list of brewing ingredients and is kept as written. The plant names are transliterations kept as written. Pala renders srang, a unit of weight. "Goat water" renders ra'i chu literally; its exact sense is unclear. The final glosses are number code words (desire 13, Rudras 11, bodily openings 9, sages 7, arrows 5) used to locate letters in a grid for extracting mantras.  The mantra-extraction passage uses a grid of letters with number code words, and "strengthening the vowel" renders the Sanskrit grammatical term guṇa (a plus u giving o). In "shes rab snod pa spong ba," snod pa is read as smod pa ("disparaging"), matching the fourteenth root downfall, disparaging women, who are wisdom. Thangphrom is an unidentified wood, kept in phonetics. The gloss "elephant dung means together with an elephant" is translated as written.  "The Blessed Lady" here is the Perfection of Wisdom (Bhagavatī). The quotation as it stands reads "that mind exists" (yod); the familiar sūtra wording says the mind is no mind, so yod may be a slip for med, but the text is rendered as written. "Is not the body" is also rendered as written; it fits the earlier refutation of anything composed of atoms.  The chapter on images (the thirtieth) begins here; the passage is technical iconometry, with "palm" (tāla) as one face-length and "minor part" or "digit" (aṅgula) as a twelfth of it. Read ཐད་སྐུ as ཐང་སྐུ, "painted scroll." "The central Vasu portion" uses Vasu as a number word for eight, as the commentary's own gloss of eight minor parts shows. In "as far as the wrist" the Tibetan adds བཅུ ("ten") after the word for wrist, which does not fit and is left untranslated. "The Characteristics of Images" is taken as a reference to an iconometric treatise (Pratimālakṣaṇa); it could also be read generically. Some of the stated sums (for example twelve and a half against three parts of four and a half) do not tally; they are rendered as written.  "Barley grain" renders ནས (yava), the eighth part of a digit, as the passage itself defines it. "Pupil" renders the transliterated term པུ་ཏ་ལི (putalī). "The mudga of the eye" keeps the transliterated term མུད་ག, whose exact iconometric sense is unclear. The description of the eyelid and eye-opening is highly compressed, and its construal is somewhat uncertain. Read འདྲི as འབྲི, "to draw." In the summary, "a quarter of a minor part" reads ཆ་ཆུང་བཞི་ཆ for ཆ་ཆུང་བཞི་ཆས.  The passage uses several transliterated Indian technical terms for parts of the face and ear (gocchi khati, kākāni, śakuli, and the comparison object berkari) whose precise senses are uncertain; they are given as written. The sequence of fractions defining the four faces of Cakrasaṃvara is obscure, and its construal is tentative. "Corners of the mouth" renders ཁ་འགྲམ, and "jaw" renders འགྲམ་པ as the text defines it.  Several measurements in this iconometric passage are compressed and their construal is tentative, especially the extent of the breast, "the third region," the widths of the foot at the ankle and tip, and the circumference and length of the big toe. "Reduce" in the verse renders བྲི as "subtract." For the foot, the words for the fingers are rendered as toes.  The verse passage on the proportions of the female figure is extremely compressed, evidently a citation from an iconometric work, and its rendering is tentative in many lines; "seat of the senses" renders དབང་རྟེན literally. In "the fifth chapter," ལྔ is read as "fifth" in context. ཐགས་བཟངས་རིས is taken as the asura Vemacitrin. ཆང་གནས, "place of liquor," is kept literal as the quoted root-text word.  ཀུན་ད་ལྟར་དཀར་བ is read as "white like a kunda (jasmine) flower," the standard simile for the white awakening mind. "The seventh case used in the sense of the sixth" refers to the locative standing for the genitive in the root verse.  The phrase rendered "the syllables hā pūṃ of the substance that lies in its opening" is obscure in the Tibetan; it is translated as it stands. The "three kinds of awakening" are presumably those of the śrāvaka, pratyekabuddha and buddha.  "Kalpa of the Arising of the Innate" translates ལྷན་ཅིག་སྐྱེས་པ་འབྱུང་བའི་རྟོག་པ, given here as another name of the Saṃvarodaya; the Sanskrit form (perhaps Sahajodayakalpa) is not certain. The closing verses play on the title Padminī, "the lotus-bearing one" or "lotus pond."  The Indian disciple's name is given in Tibetan translation (ཐམས་ཅད་མཁྱེན་པའི་དཔལ་བཟང་པོ); Sarvajñaśrībhadra is the reconstructed Sanskrit form. "The holy lama, the Dharma king" is a title, and the person is not named.

The following is a note about this translation.

This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
